Universality of Kernel Random Matrices and Kernel Regression in the Quadratic Regime
Parthe Pandit pandit@iitb.ac.in, Zhichao Wang zhichao.wang@berkeley.edu, Yizhe Zhu yizhezhu@usc.edu

Abstract
Kernel ridge regression (KRR) is a popular class of machine learning models that has become an important tool for understanding deep learning. Much of the focus thus far has been on studying the proportional asymptotic regime, n ≍ d n\asymp d , where n n is the number of training samples and d d is the dimension of the dataset. In the proportional regime, under certain conditions on the data distribution, the kernel random matrix involved in KRR exhibits behavior akin to that of a linear kernel. In this work, we extend the study of kernel regression to the quadratic asymptotic regime, where n ≍ d 2 n\asymp d^{2} . In this regime, we demonstrate that a broad class of inner-product kernels exhibits behavior similar to a quadratic kernel. Specifically, we establish an operator norm approximation bound for the difference between the original kernel random matrix and a quadratic kernel random matrix with additional correction terms compared to the Taylor expansion of the kernel functions. The approximation works for general data distributions under a Gaussian-moment-matching assumption with a covariance structure. This new approximation is utilized to obtain a limiting spectral distribution of the original kernel matrix and characterize the precise asymptotic training and test errors for KRR in the quadratic regime when n / d 2 n/d^{2} converges to a non-zero constant. The generalization errors are obtained for (i) a random teacher model, (ii) a deterministic teacher model where the weights are perfectly aligned with the covariance of the data. Under the random teacher model setting, we also verify that the generalized cross-validation (GCV) estimator can consistently estimate the generalization error in the quadratic regime for anisotropic data. Our proof techniques combine moment methods, Wick’s formula, orthogonal polynomials, and resolvent analysis of random matrices with correlated entries.



keywords

kernel ridge regression, random matrix theory, random tensor, high-dimensional statistics, generalization theory.

1
Introduction

Deep neural networks have become the dominant class of models in machine learning, breaking new benchmarks every few weeks. A certain architecture of deep neural networks, wide neural networks, is closely related to the kernel methods
(
Jacot et al., 2018
)
. Kernel methods
(
Schölkopf and Smola, 2002
;
Williams and Rasmussen, 2006
)
also exhibit many phenomena previously thought to be specific to deep neural networks
(
Belkin et al., 2018
)
.
Consequently, understanding kernel models in high-dimensional limits has gathered a lot of renewed attention due to their analytical traceability
(
Radhakrishnan et al., 2024
)
.

In recent years, the study of kernel ridge regression (KRR) in high-dimensional settings has gained attention due to its relevance in understanding modern machine learning phenomena such as benign overfitting
(
Bartlett et al., 2020
;
Tsigler and Bartlett, 2023
;
Bartlett et al., 2021
)
and the double descent risk curve
(
Belkin et al., 2019
;
Mei and Montanari, 2019
)
. High-dimensional asymptotics reveal that models can generalize well even in regimes where the number of parameters far exceeds the number of data points. The multiple descent curves
(
Liang et al., 2020
)
observed in some settings further enrich this landscape. Notably, the emergence of the neural tangent kernel (NTK) framework
(
Jacot et al., 2018
)
has provided a powerful framework to analyze the training dynamics and generalization behavior of overparameterized neural networks. NTK connects infinite-width neural networks to kernel methods, such as KRR, allowing for a tractable theoretical analysis and shedding light on how such overparameterized models exhibit generalization.

A particular line of attack towards understanding kernel methods has been using asymptotic analysis via random matrix theory
(
El Karoui, 2010b
;
Mei and Montanari, 2019
;
Bartlett et al., 2021
;
Montanari and Zhong, 2022
)
. The key argumentative piece in these results is that kernel matrices in the
proportional asymptotic regime
, i.e.,
n\asymp d
where
n
is the sample size and
d
is the feature dimension of the dataset
{\boldsymbol{X}}
, are well approximated by the Gram matrix of the input data.
Consequently, in this regime, the kernel models are somewhat degenerate and can only be as powerful as linear models
(
Bartlett et al., 2021
;
Ba et al., 2022
)
.
While this has provided us with many interesting insights, intuitions, and limitations of kernel methods, the scope of this asymptotic regime is limited. Many researchers have analyzed the more general polynomial regime of
n\asymp d^{\ell}
, for
\ell>1
, e.g.,
Mei et al. (2022)
;
Donhauser et al. (2021)
;
Xiao et al. (2022)
;
Lu and Yau (2025)
;
Dubova et al. (2023)
;
Wang and Zhu (2023)
. However, general covariance structures of the data distribution were not considered in most of the previous works beyond the linear regime. One of our motivating questions in this paper is to tackle this situation:

What is the asymptotic behavior of kernel regression beyond the proportional regime for general data distribution with a covariance structure?

In this work, we make headway into this question in the
asymptotic quadratic regime
, i.e.,
n\asymp d^{2}
. For a large class of inner-product kernels, the kernel matrices for high-dimensional datasets are well approximated by a degree-2 polynomial kernel matrix, which depends on the data matrix
{\boldsymbol{X}}
and the kernel function
f
. Using this approximation, we derive the precise description of the limiting eigenvalue distribution of the kernel random matrix under this asymptotic quadratic regime and study the corresponding kernel regression problem with precise asymptotics for training and generalization errors.

1.1
Main contributions

We study a large class of inner-product kernels

\displaystyle K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{z}})=f\left(\frac{\left\langle\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{z}\right\rangle}{d}\right),\quad{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

(1)

Consider independent random vectors
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1},\dots,{\boldsymbol{x}}_{n}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with a covariance structure
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
. Denote the data matrix by
{\boldsymbol{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
. The kernel function in (
1
) applied to the dataset induces a kernel random matrix
\boldsymbol{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
such that

K_{ij}=f\left(\frac{\left\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\right\rangle}{d}\right)
. We prove that under regularity assumptions for
f
and certain moment conditions on
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},
for
i\in[n]
, when
n\asymp d^{2}
, the kernel matrix behaves as a quadratic kernel.

In summary, we show the following three main results:

•

When
n=O(d^{2})
, with high probability, the kernel random matrix
\boldsymbol{K}
can be approximated by a quadratic kernel random matrix
\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}
under the spectral norm, where

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}=a_{0}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}+a_{1}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}+a_{2}({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}+a{\mathbf{I}}_{n},

(2)

and
a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a
are constants depending on
f
and the covariance
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
given in (
8
). Here
({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}
is the Hadamard product of
{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}
with itself.
Our non-asymptotic concentration bound works for non-isotropic data under a mild moment-matching condition. In particular, it holds for Gaussian data with a covariance matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
. The precise statement is given in Theorem
1
. The spectral norm approximation bound shows that
\boldsymbol{K}
can be asymptotically decomposed as a low-rank part, a quadratic kernel, and a regularization term. The structural result is important for understanding kernel ridge regression (KRR) in the quadratic regime.

•

When
n\to\infty
and
\frac{d^{2}}{2n}\to\alpha
, we show the limiting spectral distribution of
\boldsymbol{K}
is given by a deformed Marchenko-Pastur law, which depends on the aspect ratio
\alpha
and the covariance structure
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
. The detailed statement can be found in Theorem
2
.

•

Based on the above results, we study the performance of KRR with the kernel function
K
in (
1
) and random training data
{\boldsymbol{X}}
. Our analysis reveals that the training and generalization error for KRR with kernel
\boldsymbol{K}
can be approximated by the quadratic kernel
\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}
.
The asymptotic training error is presented in Theorem
3
. The asymptotic generalization error is characterized in Theorems
4
and
7
for different teacher models.
To fulfill the proofs in generalization error, we provide a novel concentration inequality for quadratic forms of centered random tensor vectors and a general deterministic equivalence for spectral functions of a centered version of
({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}
; see Section
E.1
for more details.

1.2
Related work

Kernel random matrices.

The study of kernel random matrices has been an important topic in random matrix theory and high-dimensional statistics. For inner-product kernels, in the proportional regime where
n\asymp d
, there are two types of random matrix models in the literature. For
\boldsymbol{K}_{ij}=f(\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle/\sqrt{d})
, the limiting spectral distribution was first studied by
Cheng and Singer (2013)
;
Do and Vu (2013)
. The concentration of the spectral norm was then analyzed by
Fan and Montanari (2019)
. For a different scaling where
\boldsymbol{K}_{ij}=f(\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle/d)
, the limiting spectral distribution and spectral norm bound were investigated by
Do and Vu (2013)
;
El Karoui (2010b)
;
El Karoui (2010a)
;
Amini and Razaee (2021)
. When
f=x^{k}
,
\boldsymbol{K}
is related to random tensor models recently considered in random matrix literature
(
Ambainis et al., 2012
;
Bryson et al., 2021
;
Collins et al., 2022
;
Yaskov, 2023
;
Baslingker, 2023
;
Goulart et al., 2022
;
Au and Garza-Vargas, 2023
)
.
In the polynomial regime, recently,
Lu and Yau (2025)
;
Dubova et al. (2023)
considered the spectrum of inner-product kernel matrices and proved a spectral universality result. Their kernel matrix is of the form
\boldsymbol{K}_{ij}=f(\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle/\sqrt{d})
whose scaling is different from ours, which is
\boldsymbol{K}_{ij}=f(\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle/d)
. Although their scaling may better exhibit the bulk information from the nonlinear function, our matrix concentration and limiting law results can be directly applied to kernel regression training and generalization errors. An example class of inner-product kernels is of the form
K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{z}})=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{w}}[\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{x})\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{z})],

where
\boldsymbol{w}
is drawn from an isotropic Gaussian distribution when data vectors are of unit length
(
Wang and Zhu, 2024
;
Murray et al., 2023
)
.

Kernel ridge regression in the polynomial regime.

When
n\asymp d
, the spectral analysis of rotational invariant kernels including (
1
), as studied by
El Karoui (2010b)
, has been applied to the study of KRR by
Liang and Rakhlin (2020)
;
Elkhalil et al. (2020)
;
Liu et al. (2021b)
;
Bartlett et al. (2021)
;
Sahraee-Ardakan et al. (2022)
. Under the same regime, kernel spectral clustering has also been analyzed by
Couillet and Benaych-Georges (2016)
;
Liao and Couillet (2019)
;
Seddik et al. (2019a)
;
Seddik et al. (2019b)
;
Liao et al. (2021)
;
Li et al. (2025)
in terms of informative and non-informative eigenstructures in the kernel matrices induced by nonlinearity. Beyond the proportional case, for general data distribution,
Liang et al. (2020)
;
Donhauser et al. (2021)
;
Aerni et al. (2023)
;
Lu et al. (2023)
provided bias and variance bounds of the generalization error for the consistency of KRR; and under certain data assumptions,
Ghorbani et al. (2020)
;
Ghorbani et al. (2021)
;
Mei et al. (2022)
precisely showed that KRR can only learn low-degree polynomials based on the sample complexity
n
. When
n\asymp d^{k}
, for
k\in\mathbb{N}
, the performance of inner-product kernel with data uniformly drawn from the unit sphere
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
has been recently studied by
Xiao et al. (2022)
, then,
Misiakiewicz and Saeed (2024)
proved a dimension-free approximation of KRR via a non-asymptotic deterministic equivalence given some concentration of the eigenfunctions in the spectral decomposition of the kernel. Recently,
Barzilai and Shamir (2024)
;
Cheng et al. (2024)
considered a non-asymptotic generalization error bound for KRR under a general setting and obtained conditions for benign over-fitting. Building on the work of
Liang et al. (2020)
;
Ghorbani et al. (2021)
,
Gavrilopoulos et al. (2024)
provided a more precise upper bound for the test error of KRR under a sub-Gaussian design. This advancement has been applied to data-dependent conjugate kernels, contributing to the research on trained features in feature learning
(
Ba et al., 2022
;
Gavrilopoulos et al., 2024
)
.

Random feature models.

Random feature models, as an efficient approximation of limiting kernel random matrices
(
Rahimi and Recht, 2007
;
Liu et al., 2021a
)
, have gained significant interest in deep learning
(
Pennington and Worah, 2017
;
Louart et al., 2018
)
. In the ultra-wide neural networks
(
Arora et al., 2019
)
, random feature ridge regression (RFRR) is asymptotically equivalent to a kernel ridge regression (KRR) model
(
Jacot et al., 2018
;
Novak et al., 2019
;
Matthews et al., 2018
;
Wang and Zhu, 2024
;
Wang and Zhu, 2023
)
, whose kernel is in the form of
K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{z}})=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{w}}[\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{x})\sigma(\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol{z})],
with a Gaussian random vector
\boldsymbol{w}
. When the width is proportional to
n
and
d
, while the random feature matrix will not converge to the corresponding kernel, the asymptotic behavior of RFRR remains tractable via random matrix theory.
Mei and Montanari (2019)
;
Adlam and Pennington (2020)
;
Liao et al. (2020)
;
Gerace et al. (2020)
;
Goldt et al. (2022)
;
Hu and Lu (2022)
showed that it is comparable to that of a linear model. Moreover,
Hu and Lu (2022)
concerns Gaussian equivalence of random feature models beyond the regression setting and
proves a conjecture from
Gerace et al. (2020)
;
Goldt et al. (2022)
that in the proportional limit, Gaussian universality holds for random feature models beyond the square loss. In the proportional regime, deterministic equivalence and generalization errors of deep random features were studied in
(
Schröder et al., 2023
;
Schröder et al., 2024
)
. Notably, their random matrix results hold under general distributional assumptions of the feature vectors
\phi({\boldsymbol{x}})
in the proportional regime, while this work studies KRR in the quadratic regime under distributional assumptions on data vectors
{\boldsymbol{x}}
.

Beyond the proportional regime, most of these results considered the RFRR with the data points independently drawn from a specific high-dimensional distribution, e.g., uniform measure on the hypercube or
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}

(
Ghorbani et al., 2021
;
Hu et al., 2024
)
or under the hypercontractivity assumption from
Mei et al. (2022)
.
Very recently,
Latourelle-Vigeant and Paquette (2023)
studied the generalization error of RFRR for deterministic datasets, and
Defilippis et al. (2024)
studied the deterministic equivalence of the generalization error under the concentration property of eigenfunctions.
The asymptotic spectra of these random features or empirical NTK in neural networks have been investigated by

Pennington and Worah (2017)
;
Louart et al. (2018)
;
Mei and Montanari (2019)
;
Fan and Wang (2020)
;
Benigni and Péché (2021)
;
Benigni and Péché (2022)
;
Wang and Zhu (2024)
;
Wang et al. (2024)
;
Benigni and Paquette (2025)
;
Liao and Mahoney (2025)
. Additionally,
Liao and Couillet (2018)
studied the inner-product kernel induced by random features in the proportional limit.

Quadratic regime and learning a quadratic function.

The quadratic regime has appeared in various tasks as an extension of the linear regime.
Chételat and Wells (2019)
analyzed phase transition behavior for the GOE approximation of Wishart distributions in the regimes where
d=n^{\frac{k+1}{k+3}},k\in\mathbb{N}
with
k=1
corresponding to the quadratic regime. As another example, the ellipsoid fitting conjecture
(
Saunderson et al., 2013
)
with a threshold
n=d^{2}/4
lies within this regime and was resolved by
Hsieh and Pravesh (2023)
;
Tulsiani and Wu (2025)
;
Bandeira et al. (2024)
up to a constant. Here,
Hsieh and Pravesh (2023)
utilized a constructed random matrix closely related to our model (
2
).
In our results, we evaluate KRR under the quadratic regime to learn a quadratic function.
The classical phase retrieval model
(
Walther, 1963
;
Balan et al., 2006
)
belongs to this learning problem. The learning dynamic of two-layer neural networks
to learn a quadratic target function has been studied by
Sarao Mannelli et al. (2020)
;
Arnaboldi et al. (2023)
;
Martin et al. (2024)
. More closely related to our work,
Ghorbani et al. (2019)
examined the population loss of random features with quadratic activation functions to learn a quadratic teacher.

1.3
Technical novelties

Paper

Regime

Ghorbani et al. (2021)

&
Mei et al. (2022)

d^{k+\delta}\leq n\leq d^{k+1-\delta}

hypercontractivity conditions

-th orthogonal polynomials

Xiao et al. (2022)

Table 1:
Comparison of related work on KRR under the polynomial regimes (
\delta\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
).

This paper advances the theoretical understanding of kernel ridge regression (KRR) by extending analysis beyond the commonly studied proportional regime (where sample size
n\asymp d
to the quadratic regime
n\asymp d^{2}
. The central contribution is a rigorous approximation of a broad class of inner-product kernel matrices by a quadratic kernel matrix, under general covariance structures.
This includes:

•

Spectral norm approximation: A non-asymptotic bound that shows kernel matrices behave like quadratic kernel matrices with correction terms, not just Taylor approximations.

•

Limiting spectral distribution: A novel characterization of the eigenvalue distribution of the kernel matrix using deformed Marchenko-Pastur laws.

•

Precise training and generalization error analysis: Asymptotic formulas for training and generalization errors of KRR with both random and deterministic quadratic teacher functions.

Compared to the existing work
(
Mei and Montanari, 2019
;
Xiao et al., 2022
;
Montanari and Zhong, 2022
;
Mei et al., 2022
)
on the precise asymptotic performance of KRR under specific distribution assumptions, e.g., uniform measure on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and the hypercube, we make no specific distribution assumption and do not require all moments of the data distribution to be bounded. Instead, we require a moment-matching condition with a Gaussian distribution. Our result does not share the same condition as
Xiao et al. (2022)
since their data satisfies the uniform measure on the sphere, whose first 8 moments do not match those of a Gaussian. But formally, our asymptotic generalization error formula in Theorem
4
, when taking
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}={\mathbf{I}}
, agrees with their result in the quadratic regime
n\asymp d^{2}
. Our result is new even for isotropic Gaussian data when
n\asymp d^{2}
. We provide the first asymptotic analysis of KRR beyond the linear regime for anisotropic data with a covariance structure. Our technical assumption is the
Gaussian moment matching
condition, which is necessary in our moment method proof of kernel approximation in Theorem
1
. It is used to explore the orthogonal properties of the Hermite polynomial in the proof of Theorem
4
. In addition, compared with
Xiao et al. (2022)
;
Ghorbani et al. (2021)
;
Mei et al. (2022)
, we impose a stronger smoothness condition on the kernel function
f
. We view this as a technical assumption that will likely be relaxed in future work. We summarized the comparison in Table
1
.

To prove the concentration result, we revisit the idea of Taylor expansion of kernel functions in
(
El Karoui, 2010b
)
. Different from
El Karoui (2010b)
, the higher-order error terms from the Taylor expansion are more challenging to bound, and new “correction terms” not seen from the Taylor approximation appear in our corresponding quadratic kernel
\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}
.
We then apply a trace method to control the error from higher-order expansion.
Although a direct Hermite expansion relies on weaker regularity assumptions on the kernel function
f

(
Mei et al., 2022
)
, without the isotropic Gaussian data assumption, controlling the approximation error of the
\mathbf{K}-\mathbf{K}^{(2)}
becomes more challenging since each degree
-\ell
-Hermite polynomial contains lower order terms and it’s difficult to argue they have negligible contribution.

Under the spectral norm, we can approximate
\boldsymbol{K}
by a simpler quadratic kernel
\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}
defined in (
2
). By standard perturbation analysis,
({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}
is the leading term in the limiting spectrum of
\boldsymbol{K}
. With the “kernel trick” (see, e.g.,
(
Vershynin, 2010
, Exercise 3.7.4)
), we can write
({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}
as a Gram matrix with tensor vectors
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\otimes 2},i\in[n]
. We then use the result of
Bai and Zhou (2008)
for sample covariance matrices to study their limiting spectrum.

Finally, equipped with the random matrix results above, we characterize the asymptotic performance of KRR. The analysis relies on the connection between the spectrum of
\boldsymbol{K}
and the prediction risks of KRR. We carefully quantify the approximation error when replacing
\boldsymbol{K}
with
\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}
in the training and generalization errors for KRR with
\boldsymbol{K}
. After this simplification, we analyze the asymptotic behavior of KRR with a quadratic kernel
\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}
. Then, the challenge becomes to establish the deterministic equivalences of some functional of
\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}
and its resolvent. To fulfill this, we establish a new concentration inequality (Lemma
30
) related to random quadratic forms of
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\otimes 2}
.

1.4
Preliminaries

Notation.

We refer to vectors in boldcase (
{\boldsymbol{x}}
), matrices in bold uppercase (
{\boldsymbol{X}}
), scalars in normalcase (
x
). We use
\|{\boldsymbol{x}}\|
as the
\ell_{2}
-norm of a vector. For a matrix
{\boldsymbol{X}}
,
\left\|{\boldsymbol{X}}\right\|
is its operator norm and
\left\|{\boldsymbol{X}}\right\|_{\mathsf{F}}
is its Frobenius norm. We use
K
to represent a kernel function and
\boldsymbol{K}
to denote a kernel random matrix.
{\mathbf{I}}_{n}
denotes the
n\times n
identity matrix.
\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}[\cdot]
means the expectation is only taken over the random vector
{\boldsymbol{x}}
, conditioned on everything else. we use
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
to indicate
a_{n}\leq Cb_{n}
for some constant
C
independent of
n,d
.

For a vector
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we denote its
tensor product
by
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\otimes 2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d^{2}}
whose index set is
\{(i,j):i,j\in[d]\}
such that
\left({\boldsymbol{x}}^{\otimes 2}\right)_{i,j}={\boldsymbol{x}}(i){\boldsymbol{x}}(j)
, where
{\boldsymbol{x}}(j)
is the
j
-th entry of vector
{\boldsymbol{x}}
.
For a matrix
{\boldsymbol{A}}
whose
(i,j)
-th entry is
a_{i,j}
, we denote the
k
-th
Hadamard product
of
{\boldsymbol{A}}
as
{\boldsymbol{A}}^{\odot k}
whose
(i,j)
-th entry is
a_{ij}^{k}
, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We will use the following equation: given a matrix
{\boldsymbol{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, the
(i,j)
-th entry of
({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot k}
is

\displaystyle[({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot k}]_{ij}:=\left\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{x}_{j}\right\rangle^{k}=\left\langle\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{\otimes k},\boldsymbol{x}_{j}^{\otimes k}\right\rangle,

(3)

for
i,j\in[n]
, where
\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{\top}
is the
i
-th row of
{\boldsymbol{X}}
, and the the inner product between
\boldsymbol{x}_{i}^{\otimes k}
and
\boldsymbol{x}_{j}^{\otimes k}
is the vector inner product in
\mathbb{R}^{d^{k}}
.

Random matrix theory.

We include several definitions from random matrix theory. For any
n\times n
Hermitian matrix
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{n}
with eigenvalues
\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{n}
, the empirical spectral distribution of
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{n}
is defined by

\mu_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{n}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\lambda_{i}}
. If
\mu_{{\boldsymbol{A}}_{n}}\to\mu
weakly as
n\to\infty
, then we call
\mu
the limiting spectral distribution of
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{n}
. The
Marchenko-Pastur law

(
Marchenko and Pastur, 1967
)
with a parameter
\gamma\in(0,+\infty)
has a density:

\displaystyle\mu_{\gamma}^{\mathrm{MP}}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}(1-\gamma^{-1})\delta_{0}+\nu_{\gamma},&\gamma>1,\\
\nu_{\gamma},&\gamma\in(0,1],\end{cases}\quad\text{ where}

(4)

\displaystyle d\nu_{\gamma}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\frac{\sqrt{(\gamma_{+}-x)(x-\gamma_{-})}}{\gamma x}\mathbf{1}_{x\in[\gamma_{-},\gamma_{+}]}dx,\quad\gamma_{\pm}:=(1\pm\sqrt{\gamma})^{2}.

(5)

Note that when
\gamma>1
, the total mass of
\nu_{\gamma}
is
\gamma^{-1}
and when
\gamma\in(0,1)
, its total mass is 1.

1.5
Organization of the paper

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. Precise and detailed statements of our main results are given in Section
2
. Additional definitions and lemmas are given in Appendix
A
. Proof of the result for spectral norm approximation (Theorem
1
) is given in Appendix
B
. The proof of the limiting spectral distribution (Theorem
2
) is provided in Appendix
C
. In Appendices
D
and
E
, we provide the proof for the results on training error (Theorem
3
) and generalization error (Theorem
4
and Theorem
7
) for kernel ridge regression, respectively.

2
Main results

2.1
Quadratic approximation of inner-product kernel matrices

Consider kernel function of the form
K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{z}})=f\left(\frac{\left\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{z}}\right\rangle}{d}\right)
, where
f
is a function independent of
n,d
.
Let
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}
be independent random vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d}

i\in[n]
. Consider random kernel matrix
\boldsymbol{K}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
such that it
(i,j)
-th entry is defined by
\boldsymbol{K}_{ij}=K(\boldsymbol{x}_{i},\boldsymbol{x}_{j}),i,j\in[n]
.

Our results will be stated under the following assumptions on the data distribution and the kernel function
f
.

{assumption}

We assume that, for some absolute constant
C_{1}>0
,
\frac{n}{d^{2}}\leq C_{1}
.

{assumption}

We assume that
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, where
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
is a
d\times d
positive semi-definite matrix, and
\boldsymbol{z}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a random vector with independent entries. Furthermore, for
i\in[n]
,
k\in[d]
,

\mathbb{E}[({\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}(k))^{t}]=\mathbb{E}[g^{t}],t=1,2,\dots,8
, where
g\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. And
\mathbb{E}[|{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}(k)|^{90}]^{\frac{1}{90}}\leq C_{2}
for some constant
C_{2}>0
, and
\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{z}_{n}
are independent.

Note that in Assumption
2.1
,
\boldsymbol{z}_{1},\dots,\boldsymbol{z}_{n}
can have different distributions. Similar to Assumption
2.1
, Gaussian moment matching assumptions also appear in non-Gaussian component analysis
(
Dudeja and Hsu, 2024
)
and the universality of local spectral statistics in random matrix theory
(
Tao and Vu, 2011
)
. We did not try to optimize the bounded moment assumption. The finite
90
-th moment condition in Assumption
2.1
is convenient for deriving a
1-O(d^{-1/2})
probability tail bound in Theorem
1
.

{assumption}

\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\|\leq C_{3}
for some constant
C_{3}>0
, and there exists
\tau>0
such that

\tau=\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}
.

{assumption}

Kernel function
f:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is a
C^{2}
-function in a neighborhood of
\tau
, and is
C^{5}
in a neighborhood of
0
.

Denote the data matrix by
{\boldsymbol{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
, where all row vectors in
{\boldsymbol{X}}
are independent and satisfy Assumption
2.1
. Under all the assumptions above, we introduce the following
quadratic kernel matrix

\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}
as an approximation of
\boldsymbol{K}
, where

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}=

\displaystyle\left(f(0)-\frac{f^{(4)}(0)(\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}))^{2}}{8d^{4}}\right)\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}+\left(\frac{f^{\prime}(0)}{d}+\frac{f^{(3)}(0)\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})}{2d^{3}}\right){\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}

(6)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2d^{2}}+\frac{f^{(4)}(0)\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})}{4d^{4}}\right)\left({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}\right)^{\odot 2}

\displaystyle+\left[f\left(\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}\right)-f(0)-f^{\prime}(0)\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}-\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2}\left(\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}\right)^{2}\right]{\mathbf{I}}_{n}.

(7)

For ease of notation, we write (
7
) as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}=a_{0}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}+a_{1}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}+a_{2}({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}+a{\mathbf{I}}_{n},

(8)

where

\displaystyle a_{0}

\displaystyle:=f(0)-\frac{f^{(4)}(0)(\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}))^{2}}{8d^{4}},

(9)

\displaystyle a_{1}

\displaystyle:=\frac{f^{\prime}(0)}{d}+\frac{f^{(3)}(0)\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})}{2d^{3}},

(10)

\displaystyle a_{2}

\displaystyle:=\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2d^{2}}+\frac{f^{(4)}(0)\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})}{4d^{4}},

(11)

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle:=f\left(\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}\right)-f(0)-f^{\prime}(0)\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}-\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2}\left(\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}\right)^{2}.

(12)

Here,
a_{0},a_{1},a_{2}
and
a
are of different orders depending on
d
. These parameters are important to yield a sharp approximation of
\boldsymbol{K}
. Notably, these coefficients are different from a direct, entrywise Taylor approximation of
\boldsymbol{K}
. In
a_{0},a_{1}
, and
a_{2}
, the first terms
f(0),\frac{f^{\prime}(0)}{d},
and
\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2d^{2}}
are from Taylor expansion of
f
at
0
, respectively. The additional terms in (
9
)-(
11
) appear in the proof when we aim to minimize the approximation error under the
spectral norm
.

Our first result is a non-asymptotic approximation error bound of
\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}-\boldsymbol{K}
.

Theorem 1

(Quadratic kernel approximation)
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
-
2.1
, there exist constants
c,C>0
depending only on
f,C_{1},C_{2}
, and
C_{3}
from the assumptions such that with probability at least
1-cd^{-1/2}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{K}-\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}\right\|\leq Cd^{-\frac{1}{12}}.

(13)

Theorem
1
shows that for sufficiently large
n
, the random kernel matrix
\boldsymbol{K}
can be approximated by a much simpler quadratic kernel matrix
\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}
, which can be decomposed into a low-rank part, a Hadamard product term, and a regularization term. This extends the linear approximation result of
El Karoui (2010b)
;
Couillet and Benaych-Georges (2016)
;
Bartlett et al. (2021)
;
Sahraee-Ardakan et al. (2022)
;
Ardakan (2022)
;
Couillet and Liao (2022)
. The polynomial error rate
d^{-\frac{1}{12}}
might not be optimal (see Figure
4
); however, it suffices to have an
o(1)
error bound for the asymptotic analysis of kernel ridge regression.

2.2
The limiting eigenvalue distribution for the kernel matrix

Since the asymptotic structure of
\boldsymbol{K}
can be represented by
\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}
, from standard perturbation analysis in random matrix theory
(
Bai and Zhou, 2008
)
, we can compute the limiting spectral distribution of
\boldsymbol{K}
by understanding the limiting spectral distribution of the Hadamard product
({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}
.

From the tensor representation given in (
3
), it suffices to study sample covariance matrices with independent row vectors given by
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\otimes 2}
.
For any
k,\ell\in[d]
,

({\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{\otimes 2})_{k\ell}={\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}(k){\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}(\ell)=({\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{\otimes 2})_{\ell k}
,
hence there are only
\binom{d+1}{2}
many distinct coordinates in
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{\otimes 2}
. We can define a
reduced tensor product
(introduced by
Yaskov (2023)
),
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}^{\binom{d+1}{2}}
indexed by
\{(k,\ell):1\leq k\leq\ell\leq d\}
such that

{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{(2)}(k,\ell)=\begin{cases}\sqrt{2}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}(k){\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}(\ell)&k<\ell,\\
\left|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}(k)\right|^{2}&k=\ell.\end{cases}

(14)

Note that
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{(2)}
is not centered, e.g., if
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
is diagonal, then for
k\leq\ell\in[d]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{(2)}(k,\ell)]=\delta_{k,\ell}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{kk}.

(15)

With (
14
), the following identity holds while reducing the dimension of the tensor vectors:

\displaystyle\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\otimes 2},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{\otimes 2}\rangle=\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{(2)},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{(2)}\rangle.

(16)

Let
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}:=\mathbb{E}\left[({\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{(2)})({\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{(2)})^{\top}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{\binom{d+1}{2}\times\binom{d+1}{2}}
. This matrix encodes the covariance information of
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{(2)}
. Under the Gaussian moment matching condition for
{\boldsymbol{z}}_{1}
in Assumption
2.1
and an additional assumption that
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
is diagonal, a quick calculation implies

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{ij,k\ell}^{(2)}=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }(i,j)\not=(k,\ell),\\
2{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{ii}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{jj}&\text{ if }i\not=j,(i,j)=(k,\ell),\\
3{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{ii}^{2}&\text{ if }i=j=k=\ell.\end{cases}

(17)

When
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{\top}
is diagonal with bounded operator norm, the matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}
is also diagonal and has a bounded operator norm. In this section, we need the following additional assumptions for our asymptotic analysis.

{assumption}

There exists
\alpha>0
such that

\lim_{d\to\infty}\frac{d^{2}}{2n}=\alpha
.

{assumption}

We assume that
f^{\prime\prime}(0)\not=0
,
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
is a diagonal matrix, and
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}
has a limiting spectral distribution denoted by
\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}
.

Our next theorem characterizes the limiting eigenvalue distribution of
\boldsymbol{K}
after proper centering and scaling.

Theorem 2

(Limiting eigenvalue distribution)
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
-
2.1
and Assumptions
2.2
-
2.2
,
the empirical spectral distribution of
\frac{4\alpha}{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}(\boldsymbol{K}-a{\mathbf{I}}_{n})
converges in probability to a deformed Marchenko-Pastur law
\mu_{\alpha,{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}
defined as

\displaystyle\mu_{\alpha,{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}=\begin{cases}(1-\alpha)\delta_{0}+\alpha\left(\nu_{\alpha}\boxtimes\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}\right)&\text{ if }\quad 0<\alpha<1,\\
\alpha\left(\nu_{\alpha}\boxtimes\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}\right)&\text{ if }\quad\alpha\geq 1,\end{cases}

(18)

where
\boxtimes
denotes the multiplicative free convolution defined in Definition
11
and
\nu_{\alpha}
is defined in (
5
). The same limit holds for
\frac{4\alpha}{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}-a{\mathbf{I}}_{n})
.
In particular, when
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}={\mathbf{I}}_{d}
, the empirical spectral distribution of
\frac{2\alpha}{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}(\boldsymbol{K}-a{\mathbf{I}}_{n})
converges in probability to a distribution given by

\mu=\begin{cases}(1-\alpha)\delta_{0}+\alpha\nu_{\alpha}&\text{ if }\quad 0<\alpha<1,\\
\alpha\nu_{\alpha}&\text{ if }\quad\alpha\geq 1,\end{cases}

where
\nu_{\alpha}
is defined by (
5
).

See Figure
1
for a simulation of the result in Theorem
2
when
f=\cos(x)
. We consider both the isotropic case when
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}={\mathbf{I}}_{d}
and the anisotropic case with

{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}=\operatorname{diag}(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{d}),\quad\text{where}\quad\sigma_{i}=\begin{cases}0.1,&\text{for }i=1,\dots,0.2d\\
1.0,&\text{for }i=0.2d+1,\dots,0.6d\\
1.5,&\text{for }i=0.6d+1,\dots,d\end{cases}.

(19)

For more simulations, see Section
3
.

(a)
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}={\mathbf{I}}_{d}
.

(b)
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}
.

Figure 1:
Spectral distributions of
\frac{2\alpha}{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}(\boldsymbol{K}-a{\mathbf{I}}_{n})
for
f(x)=\cos(x)
,
n=10000
and
d=200
, and limiting density function of (
18
) in red curves. For dataset
{\boldsymbol{X}}
, we use Gaussian data with population covariance:
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}={\mathbf{I}}_{d}
and
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{0}
which is defined by (
19
).

2.3
Training and generalization errors for kernel ridge regression

Consider a dataset
{\boldsymbol{X}}=[{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1},\ldots{\boldsymbol{x}}_{n}]^{\top}
with
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1},\dots,{\boldsymbol{x}}_{n}
satisfying Assumption
2.1
. Let

{\boldsymbol{y}}=[y_{,}\ldots,y_{n}]^{\top}=[f_{*}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}),\ldots,f_{*}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{n})]^{\top}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}

(20)

be noisy training labels generated by an unknown teacher function
f_{*}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
, and
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
where
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{i}
are i.i.d. sub-Gaussian random variable with

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{i}=0,\quad\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}_{i}^{2}=\sigma^{2}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}.

(21)

With dataset
{\boldsymbol{X}}
and training labels
{\boldsymbol{y}}
, we are interested in the asymptotic behavior of kernel ridge regression (KRR)

\hat{f}^{(\text{K})}_{\lambda}=\underset{f\in\mathcal{H}}{\rm argmin}\ \sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-f({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}))^{2}+\lambda\left\|f\right\|_{\mathcal{H}}^{2},

for certain Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Spaces
\mathcal{H}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, associated with inner product kernels, under the quadratic regime
n\asymp d^{2}.

Here,
\lambda\geq 0
is called the ridge parameter in KRR.
The estimator of KRR can be written as

\hat{f}^{(\text{K})}_{\lambda}({\boldsymbol{x}})=K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{X}})(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{y}},

where
K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{X}})=[K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}),\ldots,K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{n})]\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\boldsymbol{K}
is defined by (
13
) on dataset
{\boldsymbol{X}}
. In the following sections, we present the asymptotic training and generalization errors of KRR, given some conditions of
f_{*}
.

2.3.1
Training errors

The prediction of KRR on the training dataset
{\boldsymbol{X}}
is a
n
-dimensional vector given by

\displaystyle\hat{f}^{(\text{K})}_{\lambda}({\boldsymbol{X}})=(\hat{f}^{(\text{K})}_{\lambda}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}),\ldots,\hat{f}^{(\text{K})}_{\lambda}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{n}))^{\top}=\boldsymbol{K}(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{y}}.

(22)

Then, we can define the
training error
for this KRR as

{\mathcal{E}}_{\text{train}}(\lambda):=\frac{1}{n}\|\hat{f}^{(\text{K})}_{\lambda}({\boldsymbol{X}})-{\boldsymbol{y}}\|_{2}^{2}=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{n}{\boldsymbol{y}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{y}}.

(23)

Recall the coefficient
a
defined in (
12
). We need the following additional assumption on the kernel function
f
.

{assumption}

Assume that
a_{0}\geq 0,a_{1}\geq 0
and
a_{2}\geq 0
for sufficiently large
d
, where
a_{0},a_{1},a_{2}
are defined in (
9
)-(
11
), and
f
defined by (
1
) satisfies Assumptions
2.1
and
2.2
. We denote that

\displaystyle a_{*}:=\lim_{n\to\infty}a=f(\tau)-f(0)-f^{\prime}(0)\tau-\frac{1}{2}f^{\prime\prime}(0)\tau^{2}.

(24)

In this paper, we aim to show that Kernel Ridge Regression (KRR) in the quadratic regime can learn more complex functions compared to the proportional regime
(
El Karoui, 2010b
;
Bartlett et al., 2021
)
. The simplest setting to observe this difference is with a quadratic teacher function. Therefore, we adopt the following assumption for the teacher model, which is similar to the one from
Mei and Montanari (2019)
.

{assumption}

Assume that the teacher model
f_{*}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
is defined by

f_{*}({\boldsymbol{x}}):=c_{0}+c_{1}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\rangle+\frac{c_{2}}{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{G}}{\boldsymbol{x}}.

(25)

where
c_{0},c_{1},c_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
are constants independent of
n,d
,
{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a deterministic vector with
\|{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\|=1
, and
{\boldsymbol{G}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is a symmetric random matrix with independent sub-Gaussian entries of mean zero, variance 1.

The asymptotic training error can be obtained in the next theorem.

Theorem 3

(Asymptotic training error)
.

Suppose
\lambda+a_{*}>0
. Under the assumptions in Theorem
2
and Assumptions
2.3.1
and
2.3.1
, as
d^{2}/(2n)\to\alpha\in(0,\infty)
and
n,d\to\infty
, we have, in probability,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\textnormal{train}}(\lambda)\to\lambda^{2}\int\frac{\frac{c_{2}^{2}}{\alpha}x+\sigma_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}^{2}}{\left(\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}{4\alpha}x+a_{*}+\lambda\right)^{2}}\penalty\ d\mu_{\alpha,{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x),

(26)

where
a_{*}
is defined in (
24
),

\mu_{\alpha,{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}
is defined in (
18
), and
\sigma_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}^{2}
is defined in (
21
).

Theorem
3
covers the ridge-less case when
\lambda=0
. In the ridge-less case, the training error is
0
, and
\boldsymbol{K}
is invertible since
a_{*}
can be seen as an additional ridge regularizer to
\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}
in (
8
). Note that the limit in (
26
) does not depend on the constant and linear terms of
f
or
f_{*}
. In the quadratic regime, the kernel
\boldsymbol{K}
can completely fit the linear component of
f_{*}
even for
\lambda>0
.

2.3.2
Generalization errors

Given a new data point
({\boldsymbol{x}},f_{*}({\boldsymbol{x}}))
where
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is independent with all training data points
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}
, the
generalization error
of KRR estimator
\hat{f}^{(\text{K})}_{\lambda}({\boldsymbol{x}})
in (
22
) can be computed by

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\lambda):=\mathbb{E}[(f^{(\text{K})}_{\lambda}({\boldsymbol{x}})-f_{*}({\boldsymbol{x}}))^{2}|{\boldsymbol{X}}],

(27)

conditioning on the training dataset
{\boldsymbol{X}}
. We make the following assumption on the distribution of test data
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

{assumption}
[Test data assumption]
Assume the testing data point satisfies
{\boldsymbol{x}}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{z}}
, where
{\boldsymbol{z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is a random vector with independent entries (independent with
{\boldsymbol{X}}
). For
k\in[d]
, we assume that

\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{z}}(k)^{t}]=\mathbb{E}[g^{t}],t=1,2,\dots,18
, where
g\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
.

Note that
{\boldsymbol{x}}
does not need to have the same distribution as the training data
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1},\dots,{\boldsymbol{x}}_{n}
.

{assumption}

Suppose that kernel function
f
in (
1
) satisfies Assumption
2.3.1
and the 9-th derivative satisfies
|f^{(9)}(x)|\leq C
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
. And we further assume that
f^{\prime}(0)=f^{(3)}(0)=0
and
f^{\prime\prime}(0)>0
.

Let
\lambda_{*}>0
be the unique positive solution to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\alpha}-\frac{4(a_{*}+\lambda)}{f^{\prime\prime}(0)\lambda_{*}}=\int\frac{x}{x+\lambda_{*}}d\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x),

(28)

where
\alpha,\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}
, and
a_{*}
are defined in Assumptions
2.1
,
2.2
, and
2.3.1
, respectively.
Then, given
\lambda_{*}>0
, we can define

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}(\lambda_{*})

\displaystyle:=\frac{\alpha\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x^{2}}{(x+\lambda_{*})^{2}}d\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x)}{1-\alpha\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x^{2}}{(x+\lambda_{*})^{2}}d\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x)},

(29)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}(\lambda_{*})

\displaystyle:=\frac{\lambda_{*}^{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x}{(x+\lambda_{*})^{2}}d\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x)}{1-\alpha\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x^{2}}{(x+\lambda_{*})^{2}}d\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x)}.

(30)

Theorem 4

(Asymptotic generalization error for random
f_{*}
)
.

Suppose in (
20
),
f_{*}
is a pure quadratic function given by
f_{*}({\boldsymbol{x}})={\boldsymbol{x}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{G}}{\boldsymbol{x}}/d
, where
{\boldsymbol{G}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
is a symmetric random matrix with independent entries satisfying
\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{G}}_{i,j}]=0,\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{G}}_{i,j}^{2}]=1

for all
i,j\in[n]
.
Then, under the assumptions in Theorem
2
, Assumptions
2.3.1
,
2.3.2
and
2.3.2
, as
d^{2}/(2n)\to\alpha\in(0,\infty)
and
n,d\to\infty
, the generalization error of KRR satisfies

\mathcal{R}(\lambda)-\sigma_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}^{2}{\mathcal{V}}(\lambda_{*})-\mathcal{B}(\lambda_{*})\to 0

(31)

in probability, for any
\lambda\geq 0
, where
{\mathcal{V}}(\lambda_{*})
and
\mathcal{B}(\lambda_{*})
are defined by (
29
) and (
30
).

Both Theorem
3
and Theorem
4
apply to the case when
f_{*}({\boldsymbol{x}})={\boldsymbol{x}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{G}}{\boldsymbol{x}}/d
and
{\boldsymbol{G}}
is a symmetric random matrix with independent sub-Gaussian entries of mean zero, variance 1.

(a)
\lambda=0.001
.

(b)
\lambda=0.01
.

Figure 2:
Theoretical curves of bias term
\mathcal{B}(\lambda_{*})
(green), variance term
\sigma_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}^{2}{\mathcal{V}}(\lambda_{*})
(red), and the generalization error (yellow) from Theorem
4
. We fix
d=1000
and vary the sample size
n
. The ridge parameter
\lambda=10^{-3},10^{-2}
and noise level
\sigma_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}=0.25
. The plot reveals a double-descent phenomenon in the quadratic regime
n\propto d^{2}
.

In Figure
2
, we plot the limiting bias, variance, and generalization error curves in Theorem
4
for different aspect ratios when
f^{\prime\prime}(0)=2
and
a_{*}=0
with two distinct values of
\lambda
. This figure shows that the bias decreases monotonically, while the variance first increases and then decreases. Their combined effect produces a double-descent curve for the generalization error under the quadratic regime when
\lambda
is small.

Remark 5

(Connection to double descent and multiple descent)
.

The double descent phenomenon concerns the behavior of generalization error in the proportional regime
n\propto d

(
Bartlett et al., 2021
)
. More recently, the multiple descent phenomenon has been observed: when
n\propto d^{\ell}
, the generalization error for kernel ridge regression (KRR) decreases as
\ell
increases
(
Xiao et al., 2022
)
. Our work is related to these phenomena in the following way: we show that in the regime
n\propto d^{2}
, the generalization error is smaller than in the
n\propto d
case. Moreover, when
\tfrac{2n}{d^{2}}\to\alpha^{-1}
, the generalization error as a function of
\alpha
exhibits a double descent curve (see Figure
2
).

In the setting of Theorem
4
, the limiting bias and variance terms of KRR are (
29
) and (
30
), respectively. In the regime
n\asymp d
, similar characterizations are also presented by
Hastie et al. (2022)
;
Bartlett et al. (2021)
. In the quadratic regime
n\asymp d^{2}
, our asymptotic formula matches the proportional regime by changing
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
to
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}
. More intuitively, we showed that KRR in the quadratic regime is asymptotically equivalent to linear ridge regression with reduced tensor product features
x_{i}^{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}^{\binom{d+1}{2}}
defined in (
14
).

We expect the same asymptotic generalization error formula to hold also for the general quadratic target in (
25
) beyond the purely quadratic target case (see Figure
5
). However, it is technically challenging to prove that the effect of the linear component
c_{0}+c_{1}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{\beta}}\rangle
is negligible for the generalization error in the quadratic scaling limit. We leave it as an open question for future work.

Remark 6
.

Although
Mei et al. (2022)
;
Misiakiewicz and Saeed (2024)
;
Gavrilopoulos et al. (2024)
cover the quadratic regime, our data assumptions are more universal.
Misiakiewicz and Saeed (2024)
presented a non-asymptotic deterministic equivalence of general KRR similar to (
29
) and (
30
), but it requires a certain concentration of eigenfunctions in the kernel’s eigendecomposition, which is challenging to verify in our context, especially for anisotropic data.
Gavrilopoulos et al. (2024)
aligns more closely with our setting but necessitates sub-Gaussian
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}
, but only offers an upper bound for prediction risk.

When the teacher model
f_{*}
is not a random function but a deterministic quadratic function depending on the covariance matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
of
{\boldsymbol{x}}
, the bias term in the generalization error vanishes, as stated in the following theorem. This setting is different from Theorem
4
, since the generalization error is not taken over the randomness of the teacher model
f_{*}
.

Theorem 7

(Asymptotic generalization error for deterministic
f_{*}
)
.

Suppose that teacher function in (
20
) is
f_{*}({\boldsymbol{x}})={\boldsymbol{x}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{x}}/d
. Then, under the assumptions in Theorem
2
, Assumptions
2.3.1
,
2.3.2
and
2.3.2
, as
d^{2}/(2n)\to\alpha\in(0,\infty)
and
n,d\to\infty
, the generalization error of KRR satisfies

\mathcal{R}(\lambda)-\sigma_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}^{2}{\mathcal{V}}(\lambda_{*})\to 0

in probability, for any
\lambda\geq 0
, where
{\mathcal{V}}(\lambda_{*})
is defined by (
28
) and (
29
).

Remark 8
.

Compared to the result in the proposal regime of
(
Bartlett et al., 2021
)
, Theorem
7
demonstrates the advantage of KRR in a quadratic regime. When the teacher model
f_{*}
is a quadratic function perfectly aligned with the covariance matrix
\mathbf{\Sigma}
of
{\boldsymbol{x}}
, the bias term in the generalization error vanishes. Our result is consistent with
(
Ghorbani et al., 2019
, Theorem 10)
, where the authors studied population loss (i.e., first take
n\to\infty
while keeping the width and
d
fixed) of random features to learn a deterministic noiseless quadratic function with isotropic Gaussian datasets.
When the teacher model perfectly aligns with
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
, our result is applicable for more general data distributions.

2.3.3
Generalized cross-validation estimators

The recent work of
Misiakiewicz and Saeed (2024)
established a dimension-free deterministic equivalence of the generalized cross-validation (GCV) estimator and the generalization error, and their approximation is uniform over a range of the ridge parameter
\lambda
. Different from our setting, they assumed abstract conditions on the kernel matrices and feature vectors
\phi(\mathbf{x})
, while our assumptions are on the nonlinear function
f
and data vectors
\mathbf{x}
.
The GCV estimator
(
Hastie et al., 2022
;
Wei et al., 2022
)
is defined as

\displaystyle\mathrm{GCV_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{K},{\boldsymbol{y}})}=\frac{n{\boldsymbol{y}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{y}}}{\operatorname{Tr}((\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}})^{-1})^{2}}=\frac{\frac{1}{n}{\boldsymbol{y}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{y}}}{(\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr}(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}})^{-1})^{2}},

(32)

which does not depend on the test dataset.
With the proof of Theorem
3
and Theorem
4
, we are able to establish the following approximation:

Corollary 9
.

Under the assumptions of Theorems
3
and
4
, we can get

\displaystyle\mathrm{GCV_{\lambda}(\boldsymbol{K},{\boldsymbol{y}})}-\mathcal{R}(\lambda)\to 0,

(33)

in probability, as
n\to\infty,
where
\mathcal{R}(\lambda)
is defined by (
27
).

Corollary
9
verifies the GCV approximation beyond the linear regime considered by
Hastie et al. (2022)
;
Wei et al. (2022)
. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first GCV approximation for KRR with anisotropic data in the quadratic regime.

4
Conclusion

This paper extends the theoretical understanding of kernel methods by analyzing kernel ridge regression in the quadratic regime, where the number of samples scales quadratically with the data dimension. Through a novel quadratic approximation of kernel matrices under general covariance structures, we derive precise asymptotic characterizations for both training and generalization errors. These results highlight that, unlike in the proportional regime, kernel methods in the quadratic regime retain their nonlinear expressive power and can fully capture quadratic target functions. Our analysis relies on new concentration inequalities and moment methods, providing tools that can be extended to more general polynomial regimes. This work bridges a critical gap in understanding the behavior of kernel models in high-dimensional settings beyond linear approximations and isotropic data distribution.

Our method, particularly using Wick’s formula, is not only tailored to our problem but is also broadly generalizable to a wide class of high-dimensional random matrix problems involving non-linear kernels or polynomial functions of Gaussian-like data.
With our proof technique, for general polynomial scaling
n\asymp d^{\ell}
, we expect the
k
-th moments matching condition would grow linearly with
k\asymp l
. This method, along with our trace-based error bounding techniques, can be adapted to analyze other models, including random feature models and learning dynamics of neural networks with polynomial activations. Moreover, our approach sheds light on the structure of random tensor products, which is increasingly relevant in modern high-dimensional learning theory.

Several promising directions remain for future research. One is to extend our results to higher-order polynomial regimes (
n\asymp d^{k}
for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
). Another is to relax the Gaussian moment-matching condition to more general sub-Gaussian assumptions. Furthermore, we anticipate applying our theoretical insights to real-world high-dimensional learning tasks and revealing novel practical implications for different scalings of sample size, data dimension, and the size of machine learning models.

of Computing during the Deep Learning Theory program in the Summer of 2022.

Appendix A
Additional definitions and lemmas

A.1
Additional definitions

Definition 10

(Stieltjes transform)
.

Let
\mu
be a probability measure on
\mathbb{R}
. The Stieltjes transform of
\mu
is a function
m(z)
defined on
\mathbb{C}\setminus{\rm supp}{(\mu)}
by

m(z)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{x-z}d\mu(x).

Notice that the Stieltjes transform
m(z)
uniquely determines this probability measure
\mu

(
Bai and Silverstein, 2010
, Appendix B.2)
. For any
n\times n
Hermitian matrix
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{n}
, the Stieltjes transform of the empirical spectral distribution of
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{n}
can be written as
\text{tr}({\boldsymbol{A}}_{n}-z{\mathbf{I}})^{-1}
. We call
({\boldsymbol{A}}_{n}-z{\mathbf{I}})^{-1}
the resolvent of
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{n}
.

Definition 11

(Deformed Marchenko-Pastur law)
.

For a probability measure
\nu
, we can define a deformed Marchenko-Pastur probability measure denoted by
\mu_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{MP}}\boxtimes\nu
via its Stieltjes transform
m(z)
, for any
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-}
. Then
m(z)
is recursively defined by

m(z)=\int\frac{1}{x(1-\alpha-\alpha\cdot zm(z))-z}d\nu(x).

This is also called the Marchenko-Pastur equation with aspect ratio
\alpha\in(0,\infty)
, see also results by
Marchenko and Pastur (1967)
;
Bai and Silverstein (2010)
;
Yao et al. (2015)
.
Additionally, let us define the companion Stieltjes transform
\widetilde{m}(z):=\alpha m(z)+(1-\alpha)(-1/z).
Then, we have a fixed point equation of
\widetilde{m}(z)
, for any
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}\cup\mathbb{R}_{-}
,

z=-\frac{1}{\widetilde{m}(z)}+\alpha\int\frac{x}{1+x\widetilde{m}(z)}d\nu(x).

(34)

For a full description of free independence and free multiplicative convolution, see
(
Nica and Speicher, 2006
, Lecture 18)
and
(
Anderson et al., 2010
, Section 5.3.3)
. The free multiplicative convolution
\boxtimes
was first introduced by
Voiculescu (1987)
, which later has many applications for products of asymptotic free random matrices.

An example of this deformed Marchenko-Pastur law can be obtained by the following matrix model
(
Marchenko and Pastur, 1967
)
. Let
{\boldsymbol{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times n}
with aspect ratio
n/d\to\alpha
, where each entry in
{\boldsymbol{X}}
is i.i.d.
\mathcal{N}(0,1/d)
. Let
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
be a deterministic PSD matrix with limiting spectral distribution
\nu
. Then the limiting spectral distribution of
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}
is
\mu_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{MP}}\boxtimes\nu
.

Definition 12

(Hermite polynomials)
.

The normalized
r
-th normalized Hermite polynomial is given by

\displaystyle h_{r}(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{r!}}(-1)^{r}e^{x^{2}/2}\frac{d^{r}}{dx^{r}}e^{-x^{2}/2}.

(35)

Here
\{h_{r}\}_{r=0}^{\infty}
form an orthonormal basis of
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\Gamma)
, where
\Gamma
denotes the standard Gaussian distribution. For
\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\Gamma)
, the inner product is defined by

\langle\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\rangle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sigma_{1}(x)\sigma_{2}(x)\frac{e^{-x^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}dx.

Every function
\sigma\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\Gamma)
can be expanded as a Hermite polynomial expansion
\sigma(x)=\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\zeta_{r}(\sigma)h_{r}(x)
,
where
\zeta_{r}(\sigma)
is the
r
-th Hermite coefficient defined by

\zeta_{r}(\sigma):=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\sigma(x)h_{r}(x)\frac{e^{-x^{2}/2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}dx.

A.2
Auxiliary lemmas

Lemma 13

(Lemma D.2 in
(
Nguyen and Mondelli, 2020
)
)
.

Let
\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
\|\boldsymbol{x}\|=\|\boldsymbol{y}\|=1
and
\boldsymbol{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
. Let
h_{j}
be the
j
-th normalized Hermite polynomial in (
35
). Then

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{w}}[h_{j}(\langle\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{x}\rangle)h_{k}(\langle\boldsymbol{w},\boldsymbol{y}\rangle)]=\delta_{jk}\langle\boldsymbol{x},\boldsymbol{y}\rangle^{k}
.

Lemma 14

(Theorem A.45 in
(
Bai and Silverstein, 2010
)
)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{A}},{\boldsymbol{B}}
be two
n\times n
Hermitian matrices. If
\|{\boldsymbol{A}}-{\boldsymbol{B}}\|\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, then
{\boldsymbol{A}}
and
{\boldsymbol{B}}
have the same limiting spectral distribution.

Lemma 15

(Theorem A.43 in
(
Bai and Silverstein, 2010
)
)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{A}},{\boldsymbol{B}}
be two
n\times n
Hermitian matrices. If
\frac{1}{n}{\rm rank}({\boldsymbol{A}}-{\boldsymbol{B}})\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, then
{\boldsymbol{A}}
and
{\boldsymbol{B}}
have the same limiting spectral distribution.

Lemma 16

(Wick’s formula for Gaussian vectors)
.

Assume that
{\boldsymbol{x}}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{z}}
, where
\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{z}}]=0
,
\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{z}}{\boldsymbol{z}}^{\top}]={\mathbf{I}}_{d}
, and
{\boldsymbol{z}}
matches the first
(a+b)
-th joint moments with the standard Gaussian vector
{\boldsymbol{g}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\mathbf{I}}_{d})
, for some
a,b\in\mathbb{N}
and
\boldsymbol{w}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{g}}
. Then, for any two deterministic vectors
{\boldsymbol{u}}
and
{\boldsymbol{v}}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}[\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{u}}\rangle^{a}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{v}}\rangle^{b}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{w}}}[\langle{\boldsymbol{w}},{\boldsymbol{u}}\rangle^{a}\langle{\boldsymbol{w}},{\boldsymbol{v}}\rangle^{b}]

(36)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\pi\in{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(a+b)}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l,j)\in\pi\\
l,j\in[a]\end{subarray}}{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{u}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l,j)\in\pi\\
a+1\leq l,j\leq a+b\end{subarray}}{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{v}}\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}(l,j)\in\pi\\
l\in[a],a+1\leq j\leq a+b\end{subarray}}{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{v}},

(37)

where
{\mathcal{P}}_{2}(a+b)
is collection of all pairwise matchings on
[a+b]
, and
(\ell,j)\in\pi
means the index
\ell
is matched with
j
.

Proof of Lemma
16

The first identity comes from the moment matching condition between
{\boldsymbol{g}}
and
{\boldsymbol{z}}
, and the second one is from Wick’s formula
(
Wick, 1950
)
and the fact that

\text{Cov}(\langle{\boldsymbol{w}},{\boldsymbol{u}}\rangle,\langle{\boldsymbol{w}},{\boldsymbol{v}}\rangle)={\boldsymbol{u}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{v}}.

Lemma 17

(Whittle’s inequality, Theorem 2 in
(
Whittle, 1960
)
)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a random vector with independent entries and zero mean. Let
\gamma_{j}(s)=\mathbb{E}[|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}|^{s}]^{1/s}
. Let
{\boldsymbol{A}}=(a_{jk})_{j,k\in[d]}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
be a deterministic matrix. We have for
s\geq 2
and a numerical constant
C(s)
depending on
s
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}|{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{x}}-\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{x}}]|^{s}\leq C(s)\left(\sum_{j,k}a_{jk}^{2}\gamma_{j}^{2}(2s)\gamma_{k}^{2}(2s)\right)^{s/2}.

(38)

Lemma 18

(Theorem 1.1 in
(
Bai and Zhou, 2008
)
)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
be a random vector and
{\boldsymbol{X}}
be a
p\times n
matrix with i.i.d. columns and
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}=\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{x}}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\top}]
with bounded operator norm, and its limiting ESD is given by
\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}
. If
p/n\to\alpha
and

\mathbb{E}\left|{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{x}}-\operatorname{Tr}[{\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}]\right|^{2}=o(p^{2})

for
{\boldsymbol{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p\times p}
with
\|{\boldsymbol{A}}\|\leq 1
,
then the empirical spectral distribution of
\frac{1}{n}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}
converges in probability to a deformed Marchenko-Pastur law
\mu_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{MP}}\boxtimes\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}
, where
\mu_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{MP}}
is defined in (
4
).

Lemma 19

(Lemma 2.2 in
(
Magnus, 1978
)
)
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{A}}
be a
d\times d
real symmetric matrix,
{\boldsymbol{g}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\mathbf{I}})
be a
d
-dimensional Gaussian vector, and
\alpha_{s}=\mathbb{E}[({\boldsymbol{g}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{g}})^{s}]
. We have

\displaystyle\alpha_{2}

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{A}})^{2}+2\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{A}}^{2}),\quad\alpha_{3}=(\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{A}})^{3}+6\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{A}}(\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{2})^{2}+8\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{3},

\displaystyle\alpha_{4}

\displaystyle=(\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{A}})^{4}+32\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{A}}\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{3}+12(\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{2})^{2}+12(\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{A}})^{2}(\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{2})+48\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{A}}^{4}.

Lemma 20
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{A}},{\boldsymbol{B}}
be two real symmetric
d\times d
matrices, and
{\boldsymbol{g}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,I_{d})
be a
d
-dimensional Gaussian vector. Then, we have

\mathbb{E}[({\boldsymbol{g}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{g}})({\boldsymbol{g}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{B}}{\boldsymbol{g}})]=\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{A}}\cdot\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{B}}+2\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{B}}).

Proof.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[({\boldsymbol{g}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{g}})({\boldsymbol{g}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{B}}{\boldsymbol{g}})]

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j,k,l}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{ij}{\boldsymbol{B}}_{kl}\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{g}}_{i}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{j}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{k}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{l}]=\sum_{i,j,k,l}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{ij}{\boldsymbol{B}}_{kl}\left(\delta_{ij}\delta_{kl}+\delta_{ik}\delta_{jl}+\delta_{il}\delta_{jk}\right),

\displaystyle=\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{A}})\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{B}})+\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{B}}^{\top})+\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{B}})=\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{A}}\cdot\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{B}}+2\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{B}}),

where the second identity is due to Wick’s formula
(
Wick, 1950
)
.
∎

Appendix B
Proof of Theorem
1

To track the dependence on model parameters, in this section, we use
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
to indicate
a_{n}\leq Cb_{n}
for some numerical constant
C
independent of any other model parameters including
n,d
,
f
in (
1
), and we assume
C_{1},C_{2},C_{3}>1
in Assumptions
2.1
-
2.1
for convenience.

We first apply the Taylor expansion of
f
in Section
B.1
. Since the off-diagonal entries of
\boldsymbol{K}
are concentrated around
0
and the diagonal entries are concentrated around
\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}
, we expand
f
at
0
and
\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}
respectively. In Section
B.2
, we divide the off-diagonal part of
\boldsymbol{K}
into three matrices and control their spectral norms by the moment method. This is the most technical part of the proof. Section
B.3
deals with the diagonal terms in
\boldsymbol{K}
. Combining the three parts, we finish the proof of Theorem
1
in Section
B.4
.

B.1
Taylor expansion of the kernel matrix

We begin with a Taylor expansion of
\boldsymbol{K}
. Since
f
is
C^{5}
around
0
, through Taylor expansion at
0
, we have for
i\not=j
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}_{ij}=

\displaystyle f(0)+\frac{f^{\prime}(0)}{d}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2d^{2}}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{2}+\frac{f^{(3)}(0)}{6d^{3}}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{3}

\displaystyle+\frac{f^{(4)}(0)}{24d^{4}}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{4}+\frac{f^{(5)}(\zeta_{ij})}{120d^{5}}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{5},

(39)

where
\zeta_{ij}
is between
0
and
\frac{1}{d}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle
. Similarly, since
f
is
C^{2}
around
\tau
, for sufficiently large
d
,
\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}
is close to
\tau
by Assumption
2.1
, and we can expand
f
at
\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}
to obtain that

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}_{ii}=

\displaystyle f\left(\frac{\left\|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\right\|^{2}}{d}\right)=f\left(\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}\right)+f^{\prime}\left(\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}\right)\left(\frac{\left\|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\right\|^{2}}{d}-\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}\right)

(40)

\displaystyle+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{ii})}{2}\left(\frac{\left\|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\right\|^{2}}{d}-\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}\right)^{2}.

(41)

where
\xi_{ii}
is between
0
and
\frac{\left\|\boldsymbol{x}_{i}\right\|^{2}}{d}
. Next, we control the error of this approximation from diagonal and off-diagonal terms in Sections
B.2
and
B.3
, respectively.

B.2
Controlling the error in the off-diagonal terms

For
i\not=j\in[n]
, we have from (
39
) and (
7
),

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}_{ij}-\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}_{ij}=\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{f^{(3)}(0)}{6d^{3}}\left(\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{3}-3\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}\cdot\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle+\frac{f^{(4)}(0)}{24d^{4}}\left(\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{4}-6\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}\cdot\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{2}+3(\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})^{2}\right)

(42)

\displaystyle+\frac{f^{(5)}(\zeta_{ij})}{120d^{5}}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{5}:=\penalty\ \widetilde{\boldsymbol{T}}(i,j)+\widetilde{\boldsymbol{F}}(i,j)+\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}}(i,j),

(43)

where
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{T}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{F}}
, and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}}
are three matrices with
(i,j)
-entry

\displaystyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{T}}(i,j)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}\{i\not=j\}\frac{f^{(3)}(0)}{6d^{3}}\left(\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{3}-3\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle\right),

(44a)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{F}}(i,j)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}\{i\not=j\}\frac{f^{(4)}(0)}{24d^{4}}\left(\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{4}-6\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}\cdot\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{2}+3(\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})^{2}\right),

(44b)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}}(i,j)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{1}\{i\not=j\}\frac{f^{(5)}(\zeta_{ij})}{120d^{5}}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{5},

(44c)

which correspond to the third, fourth, and higher-order terms in the approximation error.
Here
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{T}}
and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{F}}
correspond to the third and fourth normalized Hermite polynomial
h_{3}(x)=x^{3}-3x
and
h_{4}(x)=x^{4}-6x^{2}+3
, respectively. See Definition
12
for more details.

B.2.1
Third-order approximation

We bound the spectral norm of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{T}}
by applying the trace method. For
i\neq j
, define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{T}_{ij}

\displaystyle:=\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{3}-3\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}\cdot\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle.

(45)

We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{T}}\|^{6}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}({\widetilde{\boldsymbol{T}}}^{6})\lesssim\frac{|f^{(3)}(0)|^{6}}{d^{18}}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4},i_{5},i_{6}\in[n]}\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{2}i_{3}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{3}i_{4}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{4}i_{5}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{5}i_{6}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{6}i_{1}}].

(46)

There are five different cases in terms of the number of distinct indices among
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4},

i_{5},i_{6}\in[n]
in the summation. In the following, we control each case separately.

Case (i).

i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4},i_{5},i_{6}\in[n]
are distinct
. Conditioned on
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{1}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{3}}
and
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{5}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{2}i_{3}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{3}i_{4}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{4}i_{5}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{5}i_{6}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{6}i_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{1}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{3}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{5}}]

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{2}i_{3}}|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{1}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{3}}]\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{3}i_{4}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{4}i_{5}}|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{3}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{5}}]\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{5}i_{6}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{6}i_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{1}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{5}}].

(47)

We calculate the two conditional expectations separately.

To evaluate (
47
), we notice that each conditional expectation is a degree-3 polynomial of random vector inner products. By our moment matching Assumption
2.1
, we can easily calculate them due to Wick’s formula in Lemma
16
. Denote by
{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}:={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i},i\in[n]
.
With Lemma
16
, since
{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}
has the first 8 moments matched with the Gaussian distribution, we can compute the following expectations explicitly, where
{\boldsymbol{x}}
is an i.i.d. sample independent of
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}
for any
i,k\in[n]
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}[\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\rangle\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}\rangle]=

\displaystyle\penalty\ {\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}=\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\rangle

(48)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}[\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\rangle^{2}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}\rangle^{2}]=

\displaystyle\penalty\ 2{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\cdot{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}+{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}\cdot{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ 2\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\rangle^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\right\|^{2}

(49)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}[\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\rangle^{3}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}\rangle]=

\displaystyle\penalty\ 3{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\cdot{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}=3\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\rangle\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2}

(50)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}[\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\rangle^{3}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}\rangle^{3}]=

\displaystyle\penalty\ 9{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\cdot{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\cdot{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}+6\left({\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\right)^{3}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ 9\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\rangle\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\right\|^{2}+6\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\rangle^{3}.

(51)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}[\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\rangle^{4}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}\rangle^{4}]=

\displaystyle\penalty\ 72\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\rangle^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\right\|^{2}+24\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\rangle^{4}+9\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\right\|^{4}

(52)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}[\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\rangle^{4}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}\rangle^{2}]=

\displaystyle\penalty\ 12\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\rangle^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2}+3\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{4}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\right\|^{2}.

(53)

With Assumptions
2.1
and
2.1
, we can also obtain for
i\not=k
, any integer
1\leq s\leq 45
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\rangle^{2s}\right]=\mathbb{E}[({\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{k})^{2s}]\lesssim

\displaystyle C_{2}^{2s}C_{3}^{4s}d^{s}.

(54)

Similarly, we have for
1\leq s\leq 45
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2s}\right]=\mathbb{E}[\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}\|^{2s}]\lesssim C_{2}^{4s}C_{3}^{2s}d^{s}.

(55)

From Whittle’s inequality
(
Whittle, 1960
)
in Lemma
17
, with Assumptions
2.1
and
2.1
, we have for any integer
1\leq s\leq 45
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2}-\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}\right)^{2s}\right]=\mathbb{E}({\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}-\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})^{2s}\lesssim\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{2s}C_{2}^{4s}\lesssim C_{3}^{2s}C_{2}^{4s}d^{s},

(56)

where we use the inequality
\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}\|_{\mathsf{F}}\leq\sqrt{d}\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}\|\leq C_{3}^{2}\sqrt{d}
. For convenience, we denote
t:=\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}[\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2}]
, and from Assumption
2.1
,

\displaystyle t\leq C_{3}^{2}d.

(57)

To bound (
47
), it suffices to consider
\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{ij}\boldsymbol{T}_{jk}|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}]
for
j\not=i,k
. We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{ij}\boldsymbol{T}_{jk}|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}]=\penalty\

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{3}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{3}\mid{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}]-3t\mathbb{E}[\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{3}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle\mid{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}]

\displaystyle-3t\mathbb{E}[\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{3}\mid{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}]+9t^{2}\mathbb{E}[\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle\mid{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}]

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle 9\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\rangle\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\right\|^{2}-9t\cdot\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\rangle\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2}+\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\right\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+9t^{2}\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\rangle+6\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\rangle^{3}

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle 9\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\rangle\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2}-t\right)\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\right\|^{2}-t\right)+6\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\rangle^{3},

(58)

where in the second equation, we use the explicit moment calculations from (
51
), (
50
), and (
48
). We now denote
W_{i,k}:=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{ij}\boldsymbol{T}_{jk}|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}]
for any
j\not=i,j\not=k
. Thus, for distinct indices
i_{1},\dots,i_{6}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{2}i_{3}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{3}i_{4}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{4}i_{5}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{5}i_{6}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{6}i_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{1}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{3}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{5}}]]=\mathbb{E}\left[W_{i_{1},i_{3}}W_{i_{5},i_{3}}W_{i_{1},i_{5}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{1}{3}\left(\mathbb{E}|W_{i_{1},i_{3}}|^{3}+\mathbb{E}|W_{i_{5},i_{3}}|^{3}+\mathbb{E}|W_{i_{1},i_{5}}|^{3}\right)=\mathbb{E}|W_{i,k}|^{3}

\displaystyle\lesssim\penalty\
