\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\rangle^{3}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2}-t\right)^{3}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\right\|^{2}-t\right)^{3}\right|\right]+\mathbb{E}[\left|\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\rangle\right|^{9}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\penalty\

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\rangle^{6}\right]^{1/2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2}-t\right)^{6}\right]+\mathbb{E}[|\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\rangle|^{9}]\lesssim\penalty\ C_{3}^{18}C_{2}^{18}d^{4.5},

(59)

In the second inequality, we use (
58
), and the third inequality is due to Hölder’s inequality. In the last inequality, we apply the estimates in (
54
) and (
56
). This concludes that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d^{18}}\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{6}\text{ distinct }}\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{2}i_{3}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{3}i_{4}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{4}i_{5}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{5}i_{6}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{6}i_{1}}]\lesssim\frac{n^{6}}{d^{18}}C_{3}^{18}C_{2}^{18}d^{4.5}\lesssim C_{1}^{6}C_{2}^{18}C_{3}^{18}d^{-1.5},

(60)

where we use the assumption that
n\leq C_{1}d^{2}
in Assumption
2.1
.

Case (ii).

Terms involving five different indices.
By symmetry of the indices in sum, it suffices to consider the case where
i_{1}=i_{3}
and
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{4},i_{5},i_{6})
are all distinct. Then analogous to (
59
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{2}i_{3}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{3}i_{4}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{4}i_{5}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{5}i_{6}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{6}i_{1}}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{2}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{4}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{4}i_{5}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{5}i_{6}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{6}i_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{1}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{5}}]]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[W_{i_{1},i_{1}}W_{i_{1},i_{5}}^{2}]\leq\mathbb{E}[W_{i_{1},i_{1}}^{2}]^{1/2}\mathbb{E}[W_{i_{1},i_{5}}^{4}]^{1/2}.

(61)

where the second line is due to Hölder’s inequality. With (
55
), (
54
), and (
56
), we find

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W_{i_{1},i_{1}}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left(9\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2}(\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2}-t)^{2}+6\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{6}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim(\mathbb{E}\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\|^{8})^{1/2}(\mathbb{E}[\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\|^{2}-t)^{8}])^{1/2}+\mathbb{E}\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\|^{12}\lesssim C_{4}d^{6},

(62)

where
C_{4}
is a constant depends polynomially on
C_{2},C_{3}
. Throughout the entire proof of Theorem
1
, we can take
C_{4}=(C_{2}C_{3})^{90}
. With (
58
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[W_{i_{1},i_{5}}^{4}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[9\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\rangle\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2}-t\right)\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\right\|^{2}-t\right)+6\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\rangle^{3}\right]^{4}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\rangle^{4}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2}-t\right)^{4}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\right\|^{2}-t\right)^{4}\right]+\mathbb{E}\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\rangle^{12}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}\left[\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\rangle^{8}\right]^{1/2}\mathbb{E}\left(\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2}-t\right)^{4}+C_{4}d^{6}\lesssim C_{4}d^{6}.

(63)

Therefore, (
61
) satisfies
\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{2}i_{3}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{3}i_{4}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{4}i_{5}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{5}i_{6}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{6}i_{1}}]\lesssim C_{4}d^{6}
.
We can conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d^{18}}\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{6}\text{ 5 distinct indices}}\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{2}i_{3}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{3}i_{4}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{4}i_{5}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{5}i_{6}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{6}i_{1}}]\leq\frac{n^{5}}{d^{18}}C_{4}d^{6}\lesssim C_{1}^{5}C_{4}d^{-2}.

(64)

Case (iii).

Terms involving four different indices.
By symmetry, there are only three cases we need to consider here:

(a)

i_{1}=i_{3}=i_{5}
and
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{4},i_{6})
are all distinct.

(b)

i_{1}=i_{3},i_{2}=i_{4}
and
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{5},i_{6})
are all distinct.

(c)

i_{1}=i_{3},i_{4}=i_{6}
and
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{4},i_{5})
are all distinct.

For (a), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{2}i_{3}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{3}i_{4}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{4}i_{5}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{5}i_{6}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{6}i_{1}}]=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{2}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{4}}^{2}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{6}}^{2}]

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{2}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{4}}^{2}\ \boldsymbol{T}_{i_{6}i_{1}}^{2}|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{1}}]]=\mathbb{E}[W_{i_{1},i_{1}}^{3}]\lesssim C_{4}d^{9},

(65)

where the last inequality follows the same way as in (
62
). Now, we consider Case (b). We first give an upper bound for the fourth moment of
\boldsymbol{T}_{ij}
for
i\not=j
defined in (
45
):

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{ij}^{4}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}[\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{12}]+t^{4}\mathbb{E}[\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{4}]\lesssim C_{4}d^{6},

(66)

where we use the estimate

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{2s}]\lesssim C_{2}^{2s}C_{3}^{2s}d^{s}.

(67)

Based on (
66
), we know in Case (b),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{2}i_{3}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{3}i_{4}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{4}i_{5}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{5}i_{6}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{6}i_{1}}]=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{2}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{2}i_{5}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{5}i_{6}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{6}}]

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{2}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{2}i_{5}}\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{5}i_{6}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{6}}|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{1}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{2}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{5}}]]

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{2}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{2}i_{5}}W_{i_{1},i_{5}}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{4}]^{1/2}\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{2}i_{5}}^{4}]^{1/4}\mathbb{E}[W_{i_{1},i_{5}}^{4}]^{1/4}\lesssim C_{4}d^{6},

(68)

where in the last inequality we use the estimate from (
66
) and (
63
). Similarly, with (
66
), we can also get a bound for Case (c) by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{2}i_{3}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{3}i_{4}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{4}i_{5}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{5}i_{6}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{6}i_{1}}]=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{2}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{4}}^{2}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{4}i_{5}}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{4}}^{2}\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{2}\ \boldsymbol{T}_{i_{4}i_{5}}^{2}|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{1}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{4}}]]

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{4}}^{2}W_{i_{1},i_{1}}W_{i_{4},i_{4}}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{4}}^{4}]^{1/2}\mathbb{E}[W_{i_{1},i_{1}}^{2}]\lesssim C_{4}d^{9},

(69)

where in the last inequality, we use (
62
). Combining (
65
), (
68
) and (
69
), we can conclude that for Case (iii),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d^{18}}\sum_{i_{1},\dots,i_{6}\text{ have 4 distinct indices}}\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{2}i_{3}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{3}i_{4}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{4}i_{5}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{5}i_{6}}\boldsymbol{T}_{i_{6}i_{1}}]\leq\frac{n^{4}}{d^{18}}C_{4}d^{9}\lesssim C_{1}^{4}C_{4}d^{-1}.

(70)

Case (iv).

Terms involving three different indices
. By symmetry, we only need to consider the case where
i_{1}=i_{3}=i_{5},i_{2}=i_{4}
and
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{6})
are distinct. In this case,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{1}i_{2}}{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{2}i_{3}}{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{3}i_{4}}{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{4}i_{5}}{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{5}i_{6}}{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{6}i_{1}}]=\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{4}{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{1}i_{6}}^{2}]=\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{4}\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{1}i_{6}}^{2}|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{1}}]]

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{4}W_{i_{1},i_{1}}]\leq(\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{8})^{1/2}(\mathbb{E}W_{i_{1},i_{1}}^{2})^{1/2}\lesssim\penalty\ C_{4}d^{9},

(71)

where in the last inequality, we use (
62
) and the following estimate similar to (
66
)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{T}_{ij}^{8}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathbb{E}[\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{24}]+t^{8}\mathbb{E}[\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{8}]\lesssim C_{4}d^{12}.

Thus, we can conclude that for Case (iv), we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d^{18}}\sum_{i_{1}\neq i_{2}\neq i_{6}\in[n]}\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{4}{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{1}i_{6}}^{2}]\lesssim C_{1}^{3}C_{4}d^{-3}.

(72)

Case (v).

Terms involving two different indices.
We only need to consider the case where
i_{1}=i_{3}=i_{5},i_{2}=i_{4}=i_{6}
and
(i_{1},i_{2})
are distinct. In this case,

\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{1}i_{2}}{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{2}i_{3}}{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{3}i_{4}}{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{4}i_{5}}{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{5}i_{6}}{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{6}i_{1}}]=\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{6}].

Similar to (
66
), we have

\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{T}}_{ij}^{6}]\lesssim C_{4}d^{9}
,
then all terms involving two different indices satisfy

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d^{18}}\sum_{i_{1}\not=i_{2}}\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{6}]\leq C_{1}^{2}C_{4}d^{-5}.

(73)

In summary, based on (
46
), (
60
), (
64
), (
70
), (
72
), and (
73
), Cases
\mathrm{(i-v)}
verify that

\mathbb{E}\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{T}}\|^{6}\lesssim|f^{(3)}(0)|^{6}C_{1}^{6}C_{4}d^{-1}
.
By Markov’s inequality, with probability at least
1-d^{-\frac{1}{2}}
,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{T}}\|\lesssim|f^{(3)}(0)|C_{1}C_{4}^{1/6}d^{-\frac{1}{12}}.

(74)

B.2.2
Fourth-order approximation

Now we analyze the spectral norm of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{F}}
defined in (
44b
). Recall
t:=\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}=\mathbb{E}[\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2}]
.
We define

{\boldsymbol{F}}=\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{4}-6t\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{2}+3t^{2}
.
We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{F}}\|^{4}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\operatorname{Tr}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{F}}^{4})\lesssim\frac{|f^{(4)}(0)|^{4}}{d^{16}}\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\in[n]}\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{F}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\boldsymbol{F}_{i_{2}i_{3}}\boldsymbol{F}_{i_{3}i_{4}}\boldsymbol{F}_{i_{4}i_{1}}].

(75)

With the explicit calculations in (
52
), (
49
), and (
53
), we obtain that when
j\neq i
and
j\neq k
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{F}_{ij}\boldsymbol{F}_{jk}|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}]

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{4}-6t\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{2}+3t^{2}\right)\left(\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{4}-6t\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{2}+3t^{2}\right)\mid{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}\right]

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle 24\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{j}\rangle^{4}+72(\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2}-t)(\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\right\|^{2}-t)\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{j}\rangle^{2}+9(\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2}-t)^{2}(\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{k}\right\|^{2}-t)^{2}.

(76)

For simplicity, for any
j\neq i,k
, we denote
U_{i,k}:=\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{F}_{ij}\boldsymbol{F}_{jk}|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}]
.
When
i\neq k
, using the estimates in (
54
), (
56
), and the explicit calculation in (
76
), we have

\mathbb{E}[U_{i,k}^{2}]\lesssim C_{4}d^{4}
, and when
i=k
,

\mathbb{E}[U_{i,i}^{2}]\lesssim C_{4}d^{8}
. Then, we consider the following 3 cases for the number of distinct indices involved in the summation of (
75
).

Case (i)

We first assume
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\in[n]
are distinct. Conditioned on
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{1}}
and
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{3}}
, we know that

\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{F}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\boldsymbol{F}_{i_{2}i_{3}}\boldsymbol{F}_{i_{3}i_{4}}\boldsymbol{F}_{i_{4}i_{1}}|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{1}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{3}}]=U_{i_{1},i_{3}}^{2}
.
Thus, in this case,

\frac{1}{d^{16}}\sum_{i_{1}\neq i_{2}\neq i_{3}\neq i_{4}\in[n]}\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{F}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\boldsymbol{F}_{i_{2}i_{3}}\boldsymbol{F}_{i_{3}i_{4}}\boldsymbol{F}_{i_{4}i_{1}}]\lesssim C_{1}^{4}C_{4}d^{-4}.

(77)

Case (ii)

Terms involving three different indices. Without loss of generality, it suffices to consider
i_{1}=i_{3}
and
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{4})
are all distinct. Similarly, in this case,

\frac{1}{d^{16}}\sum_{i_{1}\neq i_{2}\neq i_{4}\in[n]}\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{F}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\boldsymbol{F}_{i_{2}i_{1}}\boldsymbol{F}_{i_{1}i_{4}}\boldsymbol{F}_{i_{4}i_{1}}]=\frac{1}{d^{16}}\sum_{i\neq i_{2}\neq i_{4}\in[n]}\mathbb{E}[U_{i,i}^{2}]\lesssim C_{1}^{3}C_{4}d^{-2}.

(78)

Case (iii)

Terms involving two different indices. By symmetry, we only need to consider the case when
i_{1}=i_{3}
,
i_{2}=i_{4}
and
(i_{1},i_{2})
are distinct. Notice that for
i\neq j
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{F}_{ij}^{4}]\lesssim\mathbb{E}[\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{16}]+t^{4}\mathbb{E}[\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{8}]+t^{8}\lesssim C_{4}d^{8},

where the last inequality is due to (
67
) and (
57
).
Hence, in this case,

\frac{1}{d^{16}}\sum_{i_{1}\neq i_{2}\in[n]}\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{F}_{i_{1}i_{2}}^{4}]\lesssim C_{1}^{2}C_{4}d^{-4}.

(79)

Combining equations (
77
), (
78
) and (
79
), we can conclude that

\mathbb{E}\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{F}}\|^{4}\lesssim|f^{(4)}(0)|^{4}C_{1}^{4}C_{4}d^{-2}
.
Hence, by Markov’s inequality, with probability at least
1-d^{-1/2}
,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{F}}\|\lesssim|f^{(4)}(0)|C_{1}C_{4}^{1/4}d^{-3/8}.

(80)

B.2.3
Higher-order terms

In this section, we bound the spectral norm of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}}
defined in (
44c
).
For any
i\not=j
, we have from (
67
),

\mathbb{E}[\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle^{90}]\lesssim C_{4}d^{45}
.
By Markov’s inequality, with probability at least
1-n^{-2}d^{-\frac{1}{2}}
,
|\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle|\lesssim C_{1}^{\frac{2}{90}}C_{4}^{\frac{1}{90}}d^{\frac{11}{20}}
. Then taking a union bound over all pairs of
i,j\in[n],i\not=j
, we find with probability
1-d^{-1/2}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\max_{i\not=j}|\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle|\lesssim C_{1}^{\frac{2}{90}}C_{4}^{\frac{1}{90}}d^{-\frac{9}{20}}.

(81)

Recall the definition of
\zeta_{ij}
in (
39
).
From (
81
), we have with probability at least
1-d^{-1/2}
,

\sup_{i\not=j}|\zeta_{ij}|\lesssim C_{1}^{\frac{2}{90}}C_{4}^{\frac{1}{90}}d^{-\frac{9}{20}}
.
Since
f^{(5)}
is continuous at
0
, there exist constants
C_{5},C_{6}\geq 1
depending only on
f
such that for
d\geq C_{5}C_{1}^{\frac{1}{100}}C_{4}^{\frac{1}{200}}
, with probability at least
1-d^{-1/2}
,
\sup_{i\not=j}|f^{(5)}(\zeta_{ij})|\leq C_{6}
.
Therefore, with probability at least
1-d^{-1/2}
, for
d\geq C_{5}C_{1}^{\frac{1}{100}}C_{4}^{\frac{1}{200}}
,

\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}}\|^{2}\leq\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}}\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{2}\lesssim C_{6}^{2}n^{2}d^{-10}\max_{i\not=j}|\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}\rangle|^{10}\lesssim C_{6}^{2}C_{1}^{\frac{20}{9}}C_{4}^{\frac{1}{9}}d^{-1/2}.

Hence with probability at least
1-d^{-1/2}
, for
d\geq C_{5}C_{1}^{\frac{1}{100}}C_{4}^{\frac{1}{200}}
,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}}\|\lesssim C_{6}C_{1}^{\frac{10}{9}}C_{4}^{\frac{1}{18}}d^{-\frac{1}{4}}.

(82)

B.3
Controlling the error in the diagonal terms

Recall from (
7
), the diagonal elements of
\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}
can be written as

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}_{ii}

\displaystyle=\left(f(0)-\frac{f^{(4)}(0)(\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}))^{2}}{8d^{4}}\right)+\left(\frac{f^{\prime}(0)}{d}+\frac{\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})}{2d^{3}}\right)\|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\|^{2}

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2d^{2}}+\frac{f^{(4)}(0)\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})}{4d^{4}}\right)\|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\|^{4}+a,

where
a
is defined in (
12
). We can reorder the terms and write

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}_{ii}-f\left(\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}\right)=

\displaystyle\frac{f^{\prime}(0)}{d}\left(\|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\|^{2}-\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\right)+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2d^{2}}\left(\|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\|^{4}-(\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})^{2}\right)

(83)

\displaystyle+\frac{f^{(4)}(0)\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})}{4d^{4}}-\frac{f^{(4)}(0)(\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}))^{2}}{8d^{4}}.

(84)

And

\boldsymbol{K}_{ii}-f\left(\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}\right)=f^{\prime}\left(\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}\right)\left(\frac{\left\|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\right\|^{2}}{d}-\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}\right)+\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{ii})}{2}\left(\frac{\left\|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\right\|^{2}}{d}-\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}\right)^{2}
.

Let
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{D}}
be a diagonal matrix such that

\widetilde{\boldsymbol{D}}_{ii}=\boldsymbol{K}_{ii}-\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}_{ii}
.
We first simplify
\boldsymbol{K}_{ii}
and
\boldsymbol{K}_{ii}^{(2)}
.
Recall
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}
from Assumption
2.1
. With Whittle’s inequality in Lemma
17
, for any integer
s\geq 1
,

\mathbb{E}\left(\|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\|^{2}-\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\right)^{12}=\mathbb{E}\left({\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}-\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\right)^{12}\lesssim C_{2}^{12}\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{12}\lesssim C_{2}^{12}C_{3}^{12}d^{6}
,
where we use the inequality
\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\|_{\mathsf{F}}\leq\sqrt{d}\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\|\leq C_{3}\sqrt{d}
. By Markov’s inequality and a union bound over
i\in[n]
, we have with probability at least
1-d^{-1}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sup_{i\in[n]}\left|\|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\|^{2}-\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\right|\lesssim C_{1}^{\frac{1}{12}}C_{2}C_{3}d^{-\frac{1}{4}}.

(85)

Recall
\xi_{ii}
in (
40
) is between
0
and
\frac{1}{d}\|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\|^{2}
. From (
85
), there exist constant
C_{5},C_{6}
depending only on
f
such that with probability
1-d^{-1}
, for
d\geq C_{5}C_{1}^{1/4}(C_{2}C_{3})^{4}
,
\max_{i\in[n]}|f^{\prime\prime}(\xi_{ii})|\leq C_{6}
.
This implies with probability
1-d^{-1}
,

\displaystyle\left|\boldsymbol{K}_{ii}-f\left(\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}\right)\right|\lesssim C_{1}^{\frac{1}{12}}C_{6}C_{4}d^{-1/4}.

(86)

On the other hand, from (
85
), with probability at least
1-d^{-1}
,

\max_{i\in[n]}\left|\|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\|^{4}-(\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}))^{2}\right|\lesssim C_{1}^{\frac{1}{12}}C_{4}d^{\frac{7}{4}}.

From (
84
), this implies

\displaystyle\left|\boldsymbol{K}_{ii}^{(2)}-f\left(\frac{\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}}{d}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{1}C_{4}C_{6}d^{-\frac{1}{4}}.

(87)

Therefore, from (
86
) and (
87
), with probability at least
1-d^{-1}
, for
d\geq C_{1}C_{4}C_{5}
,

\displaystyle\left\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{D}}\right\|=\max_{i\in[n]}|\boldsymbol{K}_{ii}^{(2)}-\boldsymbol{K}_{ii}|\lesssim C_{1}C_{4}C_{6}d^{-\frac{1}{4}}.

(88)

B.4
Putting all bounds together

Finally, we combine the error bounds in Sections
B.2
and
B.3
to finish the proof.
From the estimates of the spectral norm for
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{T}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{F}},\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}}
, and
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{D}}
in (
74
), (
80
), (
82
), (
88
), respectively, we have with probability at least
1-4d^{-1/2}
, for
d\geq C1C_{4}C_{5}
,

\big\|\boldsymbol{K}-\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}\big\|\leq\big\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{T}}\big\|+\big\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{F}}\big\|+\big\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{V}}\big\|+\big\|\widetilde{\boldsymbol{D}}\big\|\lesssim C_{1}^{2}C_{4}C_{6}d^{-\frac{1}{12}}.

This completes the proof of Theorem
1
.

Appendix C
Proof of Theorem
2

Recall the reduced tensor product
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)}
defined in (
14
). Let
{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}=[{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}^{(2)},\dots,{\boldsymbol{x}}_{n}^{(2)}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times\binom{d+1}{2}}
. Then from (
16
), we have

\displaystyle({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}={\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}{{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}.

(89)

Here,
{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}{{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}
is a sample covariance matrix, where
{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}
has independent rows. We will use Lemma
18
from
Bai and Zhou (2008)
in our setting.

C.1
Variance of random quadratic forms

Lemma 21
.

Let
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a random vector with independent entries and a diagonal covariance matrix
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
, where
\left\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}\right\|\leq C
for constant
C>0
. Assume each entry of
{\boldsymbol{x}}
has a zero mean and bounded 8th moments. Let
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}^{\binom{d+1}{2}}
be a corresponding reduced tensor vector defined in (
14
) and we define

\displaystyle\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}:={\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)}.

(90)

Then for any deterministic matrix
{\boldsymbol{A}}
with
\|{\boldsymbol{A}}\|\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left|{\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}-\operatorname{Tr}[{\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}]\right|^{2}=O(d^{3}).

(91)

Proof.

We let
{\boldsymbol{A}}={\boldsymbol{D}}+{\boldsymbol{B}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\binom{d+1}{2}\times\binom{d+1}{2}}
, where
{\boldsymbol{D}}
is the diagonal part of
{\boldsymbol{A}}
, and
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is the off-diagonal component of
{\boldsymbol{A}}
. Here the matrix
{\boldsymbol{A}}
is index by
\{(i,j):i\leq j,\quad i,j\in[d]\}
.
To show (
91
), it suffices to bound the contribution from
{\boldsymbol{D}}
and
{\boldsymbol{B}}
.

(i) Diagonal part.
Recall the definition of
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)}
from (
14
). We have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left|{\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{D}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}-\operatorname{Tr}[{\boldsymbol{D}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}]\right|^{2}

(92)

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{i<j}2({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{2}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{ij,ij}^{(2)}){\boldsymbol{A}}_{ij,ij}+\sum_{i}(({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{ii})^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{ii,ii}^{(2)}){\boldsymbol{A}}_{ii,ii}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle 4\sum_{i<j,k<l}|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{ij,ij}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{kl,kl}|\left|\mathbb{E}[({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{2}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{ij,ij}^{(2)})({\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{2}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{l}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{kl,kl}^{(2)})]\right|

(93)

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ +\sum_{i,j}|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{ii,ii}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{jj,jj}|\left|\mathbb{E}[(({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{ii})^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{ii,ii}^{(2)})(({\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{jj})^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{jj,jj}^{(2)})]\right|.

(94)

Since the
8
-th moments of
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}
are bounded for all
i\in[d]
, the contribution from (
94
) is at most
O(d)
. For (
93
), when
i,j,k,l
are all distinct, by the diagonal assumption on
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
,
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{k},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{l}
are independent. We have

\mathbb{E}[({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{2}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{ij,ij}^{(2)})({\boldsymbol{x}}_{k}^{2}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{l}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{kl,kl}^{(2)})]=0
.
Therefore, the nonzero contribution of (
93
) only comes from indices
(i,j,k,l)
that are not distinct. Since
\|{\boldsymbol{D}}\|\leq\|{\boldsymbol{A}}\|\leq 1
, we know the contribution with repeated indices
(i,j,k,l)
in (
93
) is
O(d^{3})
. Therefore, the total contribution from the diagonal part is
O(d^{3})
.

(ii) Off-diagonal part.
We have the following expansion:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left|{\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{B}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}-\operatorname{Tr}[{\boldsymbol{B}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}]\right|^{2}=\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2})\not=(i_{3},i_{4}),(i_{5},i_{6})\not=(i_{7},i_{8})}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{1}i_{2},i_{3}i_{4}}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{5}i_{6},i_{7}i_{8}}\mathbb{E}[\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}_{i_{3}i_{4}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}_{i_{5}i_{6}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}_{i_{7}i_{8}}]

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2})\not=(i_{3},i_{4}),(i_{5},i_{6})\not=(i_{7},i_{8})}|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{1}i_{2},i_{3}i_{4}}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{5}i_{6},i_{7}i_{8}}|

(95)

\displaystyle\cdot|\mathbb{E}[({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{1}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{1},i_{2}}\delta_{i_{1},i_{2}})({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{3}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{4}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{3},i_{4}}\delta_{i_{3},i_{4}})({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{5}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{6}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{5},i_{6}}\delta_{i_{5},i_{6}})({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{7}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{8}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{7},i_{8}}\delta_{i_{7},i_{8}})]|.

(96)

For each index sequence
i_{1},\dots,i_{8}
, to have a nonzero contribution in

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{1}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{1},i_{2}}\delta_{i_{1},i_{2}})({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{3}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{4}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{3},i_{4}}\delta_{i_{3},i_{4}})({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{5}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{6}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{5},i_{6}}\delta_{i_{5},i_{6}})({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{7}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{8}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{7},i_{8}}\delta_{i_{7},i_{8}})]

(97)

by the independence of the entries in
{\boldsymbol{x}}
, there are at most 4 distinct values among
i_{1},\dots,i_{8}
. For sequences with at most 3 distinct indices, their total contribution in (
95
) is
O(d^{3})
. Therefore, it suffices to estimate (
95
) when the contribution of index sequences with exactly 4 distinct indices satisfies
i_{1}\leq i_{2},i_{3}\leq i_{4},i_{5}\leq i_{6},i_{7}\leq i_{8}
. We have only the following cases depending on the number of distinct indices in
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}
:

1.

Assume there are exactly 4 distinct indices in
i_{1},\dots,i_{4}
. Then, to have a nonzero contribution, there is a perfect matching between
\{i_{1},\dots,i_{4}\}
and
\{i_{5},\dots,i_{8}\}
. Using the inequality
2|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{1}i_{2},i_{3}i_{4}}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{5}i_{6},i_{7}i_{8}}|\leq|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{1}i_{2},i_{3}i_{4}}|^{2}+|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{5}i_{6},i_{7}i_{8}}|^{2}
, for an absolute constant
C
, the contribution is bounded by

\displaystyle C\left(\sum_{i_{1}<i_{2},i_{3}<i_{4}}|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{1}i_{2},i_{3}i_{4}}|^{2}\right)=C\|{\boldsymbol{A}}\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{2}\leq Cd^{2}\|{\boldsymbol{A}}\|^{2}=O(d^{2}).

(98)

2.

Assume there are exactly three distinct indices among
i_{1},\dots,i_{4}
. By symmetry, we only need to consider four subcases

•

(a)
i_{1}=i_{2}
, and
i_{1},i_{3},i_{4}
are distinct. We can rewrite (
97
) as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{1}}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{ii}){\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{3}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{4}}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{5}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{6}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{5},i_{6}}\delta_{i_{5},i_{6}})({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{7}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{8}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{7},i_{8}}\delta_{i_{7},i_{8}})].

(99)

Since there are exactly 4 distinct indices among
i_{1},\dots,i_{8}
, and
i_{1}
appears exactly twice,
i_{3},i_{4},i_{5},i_{6},i_{7},i_{8}
must be distinct from
i_{1}
, which implies (
99
) is equal to zero by independence.

•

(b)
i_{1}=i_{3}
, and
i_{1},i_{2},i_{4}
are distinct. We can rewrite (
97
) as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{1}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{2}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{4}}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{5}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{6}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{5},i_{6}}\delta_{i_{5},i_{6}})({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{7}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{8}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{7},i_{8}}\delta_{i_{7},i_{8}})].

(100)

Note that if
i_{5}=i_{6}
and
i_{1},i_{2},i_{4},i_{5}
are distinct, the expectation in (
100
) is zero. By symmetry, we only need to consider
i_{5}=i_{7},i_{5}=i_{8}
, or
i_{5}=i_{2}
.

–

(b.1) If
i_{5}=i_{7}
and
i_{1},i_{2},i_{4},i_{5}
are distinct, we must have (i)
i_{6}=i_{2}
,
i_{8}=i_{4}
or (ii)
i_{6}=i_{4},i_{8}=i_{2}
. In case (i), we can bound (
95
) by

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}\leq i_{2},i_{4},i_{5}}|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{1}i_{2},i_{1}i_{4}}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{5}i_{2},i_{5},i_{4}}|\cdot\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{1}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{2}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{4}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{5}}^{2}]

(101)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{4},i_{5}}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{1}i_{2},i_{1}i_{4}}^{2}+\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{4},i_{5}}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{1}i_{2},i_{1}i_{4}}^{2}\lesssim d\|{\boldsymbol{A}}\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{2}=O(d^{3}).

(102)

In case (ii), similarly, we can bound (
95
) by

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}\leq i_{2},i_{4},i_{5}}|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{1}i_{2},i_{1}i_{4}}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{5}i_{4},i_{5},i_{2}}|\cdot\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{1}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{2}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{4}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{5}}^{2}]=O(d^{3}).

(103)

–

(b.2) If
i_{5}=i_{8}
, we must have (i)
i_{6}=i_{2},i_{7}=i_{4}
or (ii)
i_{7}=i_{2},i_{6}=i_{4}
. In both cases, similar to case (b.1), the contribution is
O(d^{3})
.

–

(b.3) If
i_{5}=i_{2}
, we must have (i)
i_{7}=i_{4},i_{8}=i_{6}
or (ii)
i_{7}=i_{6},i_{8}=i_{4}
, and their contribution is
O(d^{3})
.

•

(c)
i_{2}=i_{4}
, and
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}
are distinct. Like Case (b), its contribution is
O(d^{3})
.

•

(d)
i_{1}=i_{4}
and
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}
are distinct. The same bound
O(d^{3})
holds.

3.

Assume there are exactly two distinct indices among
i_{1},\dots,i_{4}
. We must have
i_{1}=i_{2},i_{3}=i_{4}
,
i_{1}\not=i_{3}
due to the constraint
(i_{1},i_{2})\not=(i_{3},i_{4})
. In the same way, we must have
i_{5}=i_{6},i_{7}=i_{8},i_{5}\not=i_{7}
.
Since there are 4 distinct indices among
i_{1},\dots,i_{8}
, (
97
) becomes

\mathbb{E}[({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{1}}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{1},i_{1}})({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{3}}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{3},i_{3}})({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{5}}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{5},i_{5}})({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{7}}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{7},i_{7}})]=0
.
Therefore, the total contribution in this case is
0
.

By the constraint
(i_{1},i_{2})\not=(i_{3},i_{4})
, there are at least 2 distinct indices among
i_{1},\dots,i_{4}
. Therefore, we have discussed all three cases, and the total contribution for part (ii) is
O(d^{3})
.
From the estimates in parts (i) and (ii) above, (
91
) holds.
∎

C.2
Limiting spectral distributions

We first obtain the limiting spectral distribution of
\frac{1}{n}({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}
as follows.

Lemma 22
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
-
2.1
and Assumptions
2.2
-
2.2
, the limiting spectral distribution of
\frac{1}{n}({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}
is a deformed Marchenko-Pastur law
\mu_{\alpha,{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}
given in (
18
). In particular, when
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}={\mathbf{I}}_{d}
, the limiting spectral distribution of
\frac{1}{2n}({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}
is given by

\displaystyle\begin{cases}(1-\alpha)\delta_{0}+\alpha\nu_{\alpha}&0<\alpha<1\\
\alpha\nu_{\alpha}&\alpha\geq 1.\end{cases}

(104)

Proof of Lemma
22

From (
89
), the eigenvalues of
\frac{1}{n}({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}
and
\frac{1}{n}{{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}
is the same, up to
\left|n-\binom{d+1}{2}\right|
many zero eigenvalues.
Now, we apply Lemma
18
to show the convergence of ESD for
\frac{1}{n}{{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}
.
Notice that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}{{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}=\frac{1}{n}\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)\top}\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)}-\frac{1}{n}{{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}-\frac{1}{n}{\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}+\frac{1}{n}{\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}

(105)

where we define

\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)}:={{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}
,
and
\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}
has rank at most
d=o(n)
due to (
15
). From Lemma
15
,
\frac{1}{n}{{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}
and
\frac{1}{n}\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)\top}\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)}
have the same limiting spectral distribution. Since
[{{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}]^{\top}
has independent columns and
\binom{d+1}{2}/n\to\alpha
, by (
91
), Lemma
18
, and (
105
), the empirical spectral distribution of
\frac{1}{n}{{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}
converges weakly in probability to
\mu_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{MP}}\boxtimes\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}
where
\mu_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{MP}}
is defined by (
4
). Next, we translate the result to
\frac{1}{n}({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}
. There are two cases:

1.

Suppose
\alpha<1
, then the limiting spectral distribution of
\frac{1}{n}({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}
has a
(1-\alpha)\delta_{0}
singular part at zero. The remaining part with
\alpha
probability mass is
\alpha\left(\nu_{\alpha}\boxtimes\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}\right)
. So the limiting spectral distribution for
\frac{1}{n}({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}
is

(1-\alpha)\delta_{0}+\alpha\left(\nu_{\alpha}\boxtimes\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}\right)
.

2.

Suppose
\alpha\geq 1
. Then the limiting spectral distribution of
\frac{1}{n}{{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}
is
(1-\frac{1}{\alpha})\delta_{0}+\nu_{\alpha}\boxtimes\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}
, and the limiting spectral distribution of
\frac{1}{n}({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}
is given by
\alpha\left(\nu_{\alpha}\boxtimes\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}\right).

In particular, when
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}={\mathbf{I}}
, from (
17
), the limiting spectral distribution of
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}
is
\delta_{2}
. Therefore
\frac{1}{2n}({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}
has a limiting spectral distribution given by (
104
).

Proof of Theorem
2

Due to Theorem
1
and Lemma
14
,
\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}-a{\mathbf{I}}
and
\boldsymbol{K}-a{\mathbf{I}}
have the same limiting spectral distribution, where

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}=

\displaystyle\left(f(0)-\frac{f^{(4)}(0)(\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}))^{2}}{8d^{4}}\right)\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}+\left(\frac{f^{\prime}(0)}{d}+\frac{f^{(3)}(0)\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})}{2d^{3}}\right){\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}

(106)

\displaystyle+\left(\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}{2d^{2}}+\frac{f^{(4)}(0)\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})}{4d^{4}}\right)\left({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}\right)^{\odot 2}+a{\mathbf{I}},

(107)

and
a
is defined in (
12
). The first term and the second term in (
106
) have rank 1 and rank
d
, respectively, which both are
o(n)
in the quadratic regime
n\asymp d^{2}
. Therefore, by Lemma
15
,
\frac{4\alpha}{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}\left(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}-a{\mathbf{I}}\right)
has the same limiting spectral distribution as
\frac{1}{n}\left({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}\right)^{\odot 2}
. Finally, from Lemma
22
, the limiting law for
\frac{4\alpha}{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}(\boldsymbol{K}-a{\mathbf{I}})
is
\mu_{\alpha,{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}
defined in (
18
).

Appendix D
Proof of Theorem
3

D.1
Smallest eigenvalue bounds

Lemma 23
.

Under the same assumptions as Theorem
3
and the additional Assumption
2.3.1
, we have

\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)})\geq a_{*}-o(1)
,
where
a_{*}
is defined in (
24
).
And with probability
1-O(d^{-1/2})
,

\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{K})\geq a_{*}-o(1)
.
In particular, for sufficiently large
n
,

\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)})\geq\frac{a_{*}}{2},
and
\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{K})\geq\frac{a_{*}}{2}
.

Proof.

Recall
\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}
from (
8
).
Since
\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top},{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}
, and
({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}
are all positive semidefinite, from Assumption
2.3.1
, we obtain
\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)})\geq a_{*}-o(1)
.
From Theorem
1
, with probability
1-O(d^{-1/2})
,

\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{K})\geq a_{*}-O(d^{-\frac{1}{12}})-o(1)
.
This finishes the proof.
∎

D.2
Quadratic approximation of training errors

We define an approximate training error by replacing the original kernel
\boldsymbol{K}
by
\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}
in (
7
):

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\textnormal{train}}^{(2)}:=\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{2}}{n}{\boldsymbol{y}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{y}}.

(108)

Then we show the following approximation bound of training error
{\mathcal{E}}_{\textnormal{train}}
in (
23
) via (
108
).

Lemma 24
.

For any
\lambda\geq 0
, under the same assumptions as Theorem
3
, there exists some constant
C>0
such that with probability at least
1-O(d^{-1/2})
for sufficiently large
d
,

|{\mathcal{E}}_{\textnormal{train}}-{\mathcal{E}}_{\textnormal{train}}^{(2)}|\leq\frac{C\lambda^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{y}}\right\|^{2}}{a_{*}^{3}n}\cdot d^{-\frac{1}{12}}.

Proof.

Following the proof of
(
Wang and Zhu, 2023
, Theorem 2.7)
, we have

\displaystyle\left|{\mathcal{E}}_{\textnormal{train}}-{\mathcal{E}}_{\textnormal{train}}^{(2)}\right|=\frac{\lambda^{2}}{n}\left|\operatorname{Tr}[(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}^{\top}]-\operatorname{Tr}[(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}\boldsymbol{y}\boldsymbol{y}^{\top}]\right|

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{2}}{n}\left|\boldsymbol{y}^{\top}\left[(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}-(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}\right]\boldsymbol{y}\right|

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{2}}{n}\|(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}-(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}\|\cdot\|\boldsymbol{y}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{2}\|\boldsymbol{y}\|^{2}}{n}\|(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1}-(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1}\|\cdot(\|(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1}\|+\|(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1}\|)

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{4\lambda^{2}\|\boldsymbol{y}\|^{2}}{a_{*}n}\|(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1}-(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1}\|

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{4\lambda^{2}\|\boldsymbol{y}\|^{2}}{a_{*}n}\|(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1}\|\cdot\|(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1}\|\cdot\left\|\boldsymbol{K}-\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}\right\|\leq\frac{C\lambda^{2}\left\|{\boldsymbol{y}}\right\|^{2}}{a_{*}^{3}n}\cdot d^{-1/12},

with probability at least
1-O(d^{-1/2})
. In the fourth and the last lines, we use Theorem
1
and the fact that for sufficiently large
d
, from Lemma
23
and the assumption that
a_{*}>0
,

\left\|(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1}\right\|\leq\frac{2}{a_{*}},\quad\left\|(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1}\right\|\leq\frac{2}{a_{*}},

(109)

with probability at least
1-O(d^{-1/2})
.
This finishes the proof.
∎

Lemma 25
.

Under the same assumptions as Theorem
3
,
\frac{1}{n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{y}}\right\|^{2}d^{-\frac{1}{24}}=o(1)
with high probability.

Proof.

Denote
{\boldsymbol{f}}_{*}=[f_{*}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}),\ldots,f_{*}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{n})]^{\top}
. Then

{\boldsymbol{y}}={\boldsymbol{f}}_{*}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
,
and
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
is a sub-Gaussian vector with mean zero and variance
\sigma_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}^{2}
.
By concentration of sub-Gaussian random vectors
(
Vershynin, 2018
)
,
\|\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\|=O(\sqrt{n})
with high probability. Recall
f_{*}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})=c_{0}+c_{1}\langle{\boldsymbol{\beta}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}\rangle+\frac{c_{2}}{d}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{G}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}
.
And from Lemma
20
, we know

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{G}}}\|{\boldsymbol{f}}_{*}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim n(c_{0}^{2}+c_{1}^{2}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+\frac{c_{2}^{2}}{d^{2}}(2\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{G}}}\operatorname{Tr}[({\boldsymbol{G}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}})^{2}]+\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{G}}}[(\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{G}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}))^{2}]))

(110)

\displaystyle\lesssim n(c_{0}^{2}+c_{1}^{2}+\frac{c_{2}^{2}}{d^{2}}\cdot d^{2})=O(n).

(111)

Then, by Markov’s inequality, with high probability,

\left\|{\boldsymbol{f}}_{*}\right\|^{2}=O(n\cdot d^{\frac{1}{24}})
.
Therefore, with high probability,

\frac{1}{n}\left\|{\boldsymbol{y}}\right\|^{2}d^{-\frac{1}{24}}=o(1)
.
∎

With Lemma
24
and Lemma
25
, we obtain with high probability,

\displaystyle|{\mathcal{E}}_{\textnormal{train}}-{\mathcal{E}}_{\textnormal{train}}^{(2)}|=O(d^{-\frac{1}{24}}).

(112)

Let
{\boldsymbol{g}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\binom{d+1}{2}}
such that for
i\leq j
,
{\boldsymbol{g}}_{ii}={\boldsymbol{G}}_{ii},{\boldsymbol{g}}_{ij}={\boldsymbol{G}}_{ij}
.
With our definition of
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)}
in (
14
),

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{x}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{G}}{\boldsymbol{x}}=2\sum_{i<j}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{ij}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}+\sum_{i}{\boldsymbol{G}}_{ii}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\sum_{i<j}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{ij}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)}(i,j)+\sum_{i}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{ii}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)}(i,i)

(113)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)},{\boldsymbol{g}}\rangle-(\sqrt{2}-1)\sum_{i=1}^{d}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{ii}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)}(i,i).

(114)

From the teacher model defined in (
25
), the training labels can be represented by
{\boldsymbol{y}}={\boldsymbol{u}}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,
where, within the proof, we temporarily denote

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{u}}:

\displaystyle=c_{0}\mathbf{1}_{n}+c_{1}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}+\frac{\sqrt{2}c_{2}}{d}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}{\boldsymbol{g}}-{\boldsymbol{v}},

(115)

where from (
114
), we have

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}=\frac{(\sqrt{2}-1)c_{2}}{d}\sum_{j}{\boldsymbol{g}}_{jj}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{(2)}(j,j).

(116)

Then (
108
) can be written as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\textnormal{train}}^{(2)}=

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda^{2}}{n}\big[{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{u}}+\boldsymbol{\epsilon}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}+2\boldsymbol{\epsilon}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{u}}\big].

(117)

Lemma 26
.

We have deterministically,

\displaystyle\left\|(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}\mathbf{1}_{n}\mathbf{1}_{n}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{a_{0}}=O(1),

(118)

\displaystyle\left\|(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{a_{1}}=O(d),

(119)

\displaystyle\left\|(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}{{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{a_{2}}=O(d^{2}).

(120)

Similarly, with probability
1-O(d^{-1/2})
,

\displaystyle\left\|(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}\mathbf{1}_{n}\mathbf{1}_{n}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{a_{0}}=O(1),

(121)

\displaystyle\left\|(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{a_{1}}=O(d),

(122)

\displaystyle\left\|(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}{{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{a_{2}}=O(d^{2}).

(123)

Proof.

Since for sufficiently large
d
,
a_{0},a_{1},a_{2},a>0
, we have

\displaystyle a_{0}\mathbf{1}_{n}\mathbf{1}_{n}^{\top}\preccurlyeq\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n},\quad a_{1}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}\preccurlyeq\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n},\quad a_{2}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}{{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}

\displaystyle\preccurlyeq\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n}.

(124)

Hence,

\displaystyle\left\|(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}\mathbf{1}_{n}\mathbf{1}_{n}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{a_{0}}=O(1),

(125)

\displaystyle\left\|(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{a_{1}}=O(d),

(126)

\displaystyle\left\|(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}{{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{a_{2}}=O(d^{2}).

(127)

For the results of
\boldsymbol{K}
, we can directly apply Theorem
1
and (
109
).
∎

D.3
Precise asymptotics of training error

We calculate the asymptotic value of
{\mathcal{E}}_{\textnormal{train}}^{(2)}
by proving the following three lemmas.

Lemma 27
.

Under the same assumptions as Theorem
3
, we have as
n,d\to\infty
and
d^{2}/(2n)\to\alpha
, in probability,

\frac{1}{n}{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{u}}\to\int\frac{\frac{c_{2}^{2}}{\alpha}x}{\left(\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}{4\alpha}x+a_{*}+\lambda\right)^{2}}\penalty\ d\mu_{\alpha,{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x)
.

Proof.

Recall the definition of
{\boldsymbol{v}}
from (
115
).
Let
{\boldsymbol{u}}={\boldsymbol{u}}_{1}+{\boldsymbol{u}}_{2}
where

{\boldsymbol{u}}_{1}=c_{0}\mathbf{1}_{n}+c_{1}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}},\quad{\boldsymbol{u}}_{2}=\frac{\sqrt{2}c_{2}}{d}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}{\boldsymbol{g}}-{\boldsymbol{v}}.

Denote
\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{(2)}=\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n}
. We have the following decomposition:

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{u}}

\displaystyle={\boldsymbol{u}}_{2}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{(2)}\right)^{-2}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{2}+{\boldsymbol{u}}_{1}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{(2)}\right)^{-2}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{1}+2{\boldsymbol{u}}_{1}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{(2)}\right)^{-2}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{2}

(128)

\displaystyle=:S_{2}+S_{1}+S_{3},

(129)

where, by Cauchy’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle S_{3}:=2{\boldsymbol{u}}_{1}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{(2)}\right)^{-2}{\boldsymbol{u}}_{2}\leq 2\sqrt{S_{1}S_{2}}.

(130)

Step 1: Computing
S_{2}
.

We first estimate
\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\|
. From (
116
),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}}\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{G}}}[{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}^{8}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{d^{4}}\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}}\left(d^{-1}\sum_{j\in[d]}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}(j)^{4}\right)^{4}\lesssim d^{-4}\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}}\left(d^{-1}\sum_{j}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}(j)^{16}\right)\lesssim d^{-4},

(131)

where the last line is due to Jensen’s inequality. Therefore with probability at least
1-d^{-3}
,
|{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}|\leq d^{-1/8}
. Taking a union bound over
i\in[n]
, we have with probability at least
1-d^{-1}
,

\displaystyle\left\|{\boldsymbol{v}}\right\|=O(d^{7/8}).

(132)

We can decompose
S_{2}
as

\displaystyle S_{2}

\displaystyle=S_{2}^{\prime}+{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{(2)}\right)^{-2}{\boldsymbol{v}}-2{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{(2)}\right)^{-2}\frac{\sqrt{2}c_{2}}{d}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}{\boldsymbol{g}},

(133)

where

S_{2}^{\prime}={\boldsymbol{g}}^{\top}\left(\frac{2c_{2}^{2}}{d^{2}}{{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}\right){\boldsymbol{g}}
,
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{g}}}[S_{2}^{\prime}]=\frac{2c_{2}^{2}}{d^{2}}\operatorname{Tr}\left[(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}{{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}\right].

(134)

With (
120
), we can apply Hanson-Wright inequality
(
Vershynin, 2018
)
to obtain

\frac{1}{n}S_{2}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{2c_{2}^{2}}{d^{2}}\operatorname{Tr}\left[(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}{{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}\right]\to 0

with high probability. From the limiting spectral distribution of
\frac{4\alpha}{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}-a{\mathbf{I}})
shown in Theorem
2
, we have the following convergence in probability holds:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{2c_{2}^{2}}{d^{2}a_{2}}\operatorname{Tr}\left[(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}-a{\mathbf{I}}_{n})\right]\to\int\frac{\frac{c_{2}^{2}}{\alpha}x}{\left(\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(0)x}{4\alpha}+a_{*}+\lambda\right)^{2}}\penalty\ d\mu_{\alpha,{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x).

(135)

Moreover, due to (
119
) and (
118
),

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{2c_{2}^{2}}{d^{2}a_{2}}\left[(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}-a{\mathbf{I}}_{n})\right]-\frac{2c_{2}^{2}}{d^{2}}\operatorname{Tr}\left[(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}{{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}\right]

(136)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\cdot\frac{2c_{2}^{2}}{d^{2}}\operatorname{Tr}\left[(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}_{\lambda})^{-2}\left(\frac{a_{0}}{a_{2}}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}+\frac{a_{1}}{a_{2}}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}\right)\right]=o(1).

(137)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}S_{2}^{\prime}\to\int\frac{\frac{c_{2}^{2}}{\alpha}x}{\left(\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(0)x}{4\alpha}+a_{*}+\lambda\right)^{2}}\penalty\ d\mu_{\alpha,{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x)

(138)

in probability. With (
132
), we have with high probability,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{(2)}\right)^{-2}{\boldsymbol{v}}=O(d^{-1/4}),\quad 2{\boldsymbol{v}}^{\top}\left(\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{(2)}\right)^{-2}\frac{\sqrt{2}c_{2}}{d}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}{\boldsymbol{g}}=O(d^{-1/8}),

(139)

where we use Cauchy’s inequality and (
138
). Then from (
133
), we have in probability,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}S_{2}\to\int\frac{\frac{c_{2}^{2}}{\alpha}x}{\left(\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(0)x}{4\alpha}+a_{*}+\lambda\right)^{2}}\penalty\ d\mu_{\alpha,{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x).

(140)

Step 2: Controlling
S_{1}
.

By Cauchy’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}S_{1}\leq\frac{2c_{0}^{2}}{n}\mathbf{1}_{n}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}\mathbf{1}_{n}+\frac{2c_{1}^{2}}{n}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}.

(141)

For the first term on the right-hand side, we have

\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}^{2}}{n}\mathbf{1}_{n}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}\mathbf{1}_{n}=\frac{c_{0}^{2}}{n}\operatorname{Tr}[(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}\mathbf{1}_{n}\mathbf{1}_{n}^{\top}]

(142)

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{c_{0}^{2}}{n}\|(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}\mathbf{1}_{n}\mathbf{1}_{n}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}\|

(143)

\displaystyle\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{2c_{0}^{2}}{a_{*}n}\|(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}\mathbf{1}_{n}\mathbf{1}_{n}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}\|\leq\frac{2c_{0}^{2}}{a_{*}a_{0}n}=O(n^{-1}),

(144)

where in the first identity, we use the fact
\mathbf{1}_{n}\mathbf{1}_{n}^{\top}
is rank-1, and the last inequality is due to (
118
). For the second term, we have

\displaystyle\frac{2c_{1}^{2}}{n}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\beta}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{1}{n}\|(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{X}}\|^{2}

(145)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{na_{*}}\left\|(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-1/2}{\boldsymbol{X}}\right\|^{2}=O(d/n),

(146)

where the last inequality is due to (
119
). Therefore
\frac{1}{n}{S_{1}}=o(1)
with high probability. Combining the estimates of
S_{1},S_{2}
, Lemma
27
holds due to (
140
), (
129
), and (
130
).
∎

Lemma 28
.

Under the same assumptions as Theorem
3
, the following holds with high probability:

\left|\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}-\frac{\sigma_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}^{2}}{n}\operatorname{Tr}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}\right|=o(1)
.
And in probability,

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}^{2}}{n}\operatorname{Tr}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}\to\int\frac{\sigma_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}^{2}}{\left(\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}{4\alpha}x+a_{*}+\lambda\right)^{2}}\penalty\ d\mu_{\alpha,{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x).

(147)

Proof.

The first claim follows from Hanson-Wright inequality for sub-Gaussian random vectors in
(
Rudelson and Vershynin, 2013
)
since
\boldsymbol{\epsilon}
is sub-Gaussian and (
109
) holds with high probability. From Theorem
2
,
the empirical spectral distribution of
\frac{4\alpha}{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}-a{\mathbf{I}}_{n})
converges to
\mu_{\alpha,{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}
. Take a test function
\frac{1}{(x+a_{*}+\lambda)^{2}}
which is bounded continuous on interval
[-a_{*}/2,\infty)
. From Lemma
23
, for sufficiently large
n
,
\lambda_{\min}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}-a{\mathbf{I}}_{n})\geq-\frac{a_{*}}{2}
. Therefore, (
147
) holds from weak convergence.
∎

Lemma 29
.

Under the same assumptions as Theorem
3
, with high probability,

\frac{1}{n}\boldsymbol{\epsilon}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{u}}=o(1).

Proof.

We do a second-moment estimate. Note that

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}\left(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{u}}\right)^{2}=\sigma_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}^{2}{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-4}{\boldsymbol{u}}.

Applying the same proof as in Lemma
27
, one can show that

\frac{\sigma_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}^{2}}{n}{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-4}{\boldsymbol{u}}

converges in probability to a deterministic limit. Therefore, with high probability, we have

\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}\left(\boldsymbol{\epsilon}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}^{(2)}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}}_{n})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{u}}\right)^{2}=O(n)
.
Hence, Lemma
29
holds by Markov’s inequality.
∎

Proof of Theorem
3

From (
112
), it suffices to analyze the asymptotic behavior of
{\mathcal{E}}_{\textnormal{train}}^{(2)}
. Therefore, from the decomposition of
{\mathcal{E}}_{\textnormal{train}}^{(2)}
in (
117
), with Lemmas
27
,
28
, and
29
, we have

{\mathcal{E}}_{\textnormal{train}}\to\lambda^{2}\int\frac{\frac{c_{2}^{2}}{\alpha}x+\sigma_{\boldsymbol{\epsilon}}^{2}}{\left(\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}{4\alpha}x+a_{*}+\lambda\right)^{2}}\penalty\ d\mu_{\alpha,{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x)

in probability. This finishes the proof.

Appendix E
The analysis of generalization errors

E.1
Preliminary calculations

E.1.1
Concentration of random quadratic forms

The following lemma improves the second moment estimate in (
91
).

Lemma 30
.

Assume
{\boldsymbol{x}}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{z}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}
is diagonal and bounded in operator norm.
{\boldsymbol{z}}
has independent entries with 1st, 3rd, and 5th moments zero, and each entry has finite first 56-th moments. We have for any deterministic matrix
{\boldsymbol{A}}\in\mathbb{R}^{\binom{d+1}{2}\times\binom{d+1}{2}}
with
\|{\boldsymbol{A}}\|\leq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left|{\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}-\operatorname{Tr}[{\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}]\right|^{14}=O(d^{25.5}).

(148)

And under the Assumption
2.1
for
{\boldsymbol{X}}
, for all
i\in[n]
, with probability at least
1-O(d^{-\frac{1}{5}})
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\left|{\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{A}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)}-\operatorname{Tr}[{\boldsymbol{A}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}]\right|=O(n^{-\frac{1}{60}}).

(149)

Proof.

We first focus on proving (
148
). For ease of notation, in this proof, we denote
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}
as the
i
-th entry of
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
for
i\in[d]
.
We decompose
{\boldsymbol{A}}={\boldsymbol{D}}+{\boldsymbol{B}}
, where
{\boldsymbol{D}}
is the diagonal part of
{\boldsymbol{A}}
and
{\boldsymbol{B}}
is the off-diagonal part of
{\boldsymbol{A}}
, and compute their contribution below.

(i) Diagonal part.
Following the same argument as in the proof of Lemma
22
, recall the definition of
\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}
from (
90
), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left|{\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{D}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}-\operatorname{Tr}[{\boldsymbol{D}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}]\right|^{14}

(150)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{i<j}2({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{2}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{ij,ij}^{(2)}){\boldsymbol{A}}_{ij,ij}+\sum_{i}(({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{ii})^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{ii,ii}^{(2)}){\boldsymbol{A}}_{ii,ii}\right)^{14}

(151)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{i<j}({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{2}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{ij,ij}^{(2)}){\boldsymbol{A}}_{ij,ij}\right)^{14}+\mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{i}(({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{ii})^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{ii,ii}^{(2)}){\boldsymbol{A}}_{ii,ii}\right)^{14}.

(152)

For the second term in (
152
), by independence of entries in
{\boldsymbol{x}}
, its contribution is
O(d^{14})
.
We now expand the first term in (
152
), which gives

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1}<j_{1},\cdots,i_{14}<j_{14}}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{1}j_{1},i_{1}j_{1}}\cdots{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{14}j_{14},i_{14},j_{14}}\mathbb{E}\left[(\boldsymbol{x}_{i_{1}}^{2}\boldsymbol{x}_{j_{1}}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{1}j_{1},i_{1}j_{1}}^{(2)})\cdots(\boldsymbol{x}_{i_{14}}^{2}\boldsymbol{x}_{j_{14}}^{2}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{14}j_{14},i_{14}j_{14}}^{(2)})\right].\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\

(153)

Since each product in the expectation is centered, to have a nonzero expectation in (
153
), each pair in
\{i_{1},j_{1}\},\cdots\{i_{14},j_{14}\}
must have at least one index with multiplicity at least 2.
We now divide 14 pairs
\{i_{1},j_{1}\},\cdots\{i_{14},j_{14}\}
into 7 groups of 4 indices given by

\{i_{1},j_{1},i_{2},j_{2}\},\dots,\{i_{13},j_{13},i_{14},j_{14}\}.

To have zero expectation in (
153
), we claim there are at most 21 distinct indices in
i_{1},j_{1}\dots,i_{14},j_{14}
. Otherwise, at least one group of indices only appears once. This gives zero expectation in (
153
), a contradiction. Hence, in (
153
), the total contribution is
O(d^{21})
.
Combining the two terms in (
152
), the total contribution is
O(d^{21})
.

(ii) Off-diagonal part.

Now we do the following expansion:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left|{\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{B}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}-\operatorname{Tr}[{\boldsymbol{B}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}]\right|^{14}=\penalty\ \mathbb{E}\left(\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2})\not=(i_{3},i_{4})}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{1}i_{2},i_{3}i_{4}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}_{i_{3}i_{4}}\right)^{14}

(154)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2})\not=(i_{3},i_{4}),\cdots,(i_{53},i_{54})\not=(i_{55},i_{56})}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{1}i_{2},i_{3}i_{4}}\cdots{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{53}i_{54},i_{55}i_{56}}\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}_{i_{3}i_{4}}\cdots\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}_{i_{53}i_{54}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}_{i_{55}i_{56}}\right]

(155)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{(i_{1},i_{2})\not=(i_{3},i_{4}),\cdots,(i_{53},i_{54})\not=(i_{55},i_{56})}|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{1}i_{2},i_{3}i_{4}}\cdots{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{53}i_{54},i_{55}i_{56}}|\left|\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}_{i_{3}i_{4}}\cdots\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}_{i_{53}i_{54}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}_{i_{55}i_{56}}\right]\right|.

(156)

And

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}_{i_{1}i_{2}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}_{i_{3}i_{4}}\cdots\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}_{i_{53}i_{54}}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}^{(2)}_{i_{55}i_{56}}\right]

(157)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ \mathbb{E}\left[({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{1}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{2}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{1},i_{2}}\delta_{i_{1},i_{2}})({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{3}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{4}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{3},i_{4}}\delta_{i_{3},i_{4}})\cdots({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{55}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i_{56}}-{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{i_{55},i_{56}}\delta_{i_{55},i_{56}})\right],

(158)

with the restriction that

\displaystyle i_{1}\leq i_{2},\dots,i_{55}\leq i_{56},\quad(i_{1},i_{2})\not=(i_{3},i_{4}),\cdots,(i_{53},i_{54})\not=(i_{55},i_{56}).

(159)

We estimate (
154
) with the following three steps.

Step 1: Preliminary estimates.

Suppose
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}
are 4 distinct indices, then by Cauchy’s inequality and the fact that
\|{\boldsymbol{A}}\|_{\mathsf{F}}\leq\sqrt{\binom{d+1}{2}}\|{\boldsymbol{A}}\|_{\mathsf{F}}\leq d
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\in[d],\text{ 4 distinct indices}}|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{1}i_{2},i_{3}i_{4}}|\leq\sqrt{d^{4}\|{\boldsymbol{A}}\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{2}}\leq d^{3}.

(160)

Similarly, if there are at most 3 distinct indices among
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\in[d]
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\in[d],\text{ 3 distinct indices}}|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{1}i_{2},i_{3}i_{4}}|\leq\sqrt{d^{3}\|{\boldsymbol{A}}\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{2}}\leq d^{2.5}.

(161)

If there are two distinct indices, due to the restriction (
159
), the entries must be
A_{i_{1}i_{1},i_{2}i_{2}}
with
i_{1}\not=i_{2}
, and we have from Cauchy’s inequality,

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1},i_{2}}|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{1}i_{1},i_{2}i_{2}}|\leq\sqrt{d^{2}\|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{S}\|_{\mathsf{F}}^{2}}\leq d^{1.5},

(162)

where
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{S}
is a
d\times d
submatrix of
{\boldsymbol{A}}
given by
{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{1}i_{1},i_{2}i_{2}}
and we use the fact that
\|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{S}\|_{\mathsf{F}}\leq\sqrt{d}\|{\boldsymbol{A}}\|\leq\sqrt{d}
. We also have the following trivial bound for all
i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\in[d]
:

\displaystyle|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{1}i_{2},i_{3}i_{4}}|\leq\|{\boldsymbol{A}}\|\leq 1.

(163)

By the independence of entries in
{\boldsymbol{x}}
, to have a nonzero expectation in (
157
), there are at most 28 distinct indices in
i_{1},\dots,i_{56}
. On the other hand, if there are at most
25
distinct indices, the total contribution for those terms is at most
O(d^{25})
. Therefore, to show (
148
), we only need to consider
(i_{1},\dots,i_{56})
where there are
26,27
or
28
many distinct indices.

We group the 56 indices into 14 tuples:
(i_{4k-3},i_{4k-2},i_{4k-1},i_{4k})
for
1\leq k\leq 14
. To have a nonzero zero expectation in (
157
), with the restriction from (
159
), there are at least 2 distinct indices in each tuple
(i_{4k-3},i_{4k-2},i_{4k-1},i_{4k})
for
1\leq k\leq 14
.
Among the 14 tuples, we define a subset called
good tuples
recursively. The first good tuple is
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})
. If there are
s
many distinct indices in
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})
for
s=2,3,4
, we call
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})
a
good
s
-tuple
. According to the lexicographic order, the next tuple that does not share any common indices with previous good tuples is also a good
s
-tuple if it has
s
distinct indices.

Step 2: An algorithm to bound (
154
).
We now describe an algorithm to provide a bound on (
154
) with the following steps to bound the contribution from each tuple. The strategy is to use the better bounds (
160
), (
161
), and (
162
) as many times as possible.

•

Start with the first good tuple
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})
. Track all the tuples which coincide with at least one index in
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})
.
Bound the contribution from all tuples which shared at least one indices with
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})
in (
154
) using (
163
) and bound the contribution of
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})
using (
160
), (
161
), or (
162
) depending on the number of distinct indices
s
. Without loss of generality, we may assume the second to the
(s+1)
-th tuples in lexicographical order share indices with the first tuple.
See Figure
6
for an example when
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})
is a good 3-tuple. In the case of Figure
6
, We can bound

\displaystyle\sum_{i_{1},i_{2},\dots,i_{10}}|{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{1}i_{2},i_{3}i_{4}}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{5}i_{6},i_{7}i_{8}}{\boldsymbol{A}}_{i_{9}i_{10},i_{11}i_{12}}|\leq

\displaystyle d^{2.5}\left(\sum_{i_{6},i_{7},i_{8},i_{9},i_{11},i_{12}}1\right).

(164)

by using (
161
), which reduces the sum of 10 indices to a sum of 6 indices.

1

In this example, the tuple
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})
share common indices with two tuples
(i_{5},i_{6},i_{7},i_{8})
and
(i_{9},i_{10},i_{11},i_{12})
by identifying
i_{1}=i_{2},i_{3}=i_{5},i_{4}=i_{10}
. The relations among
i_{6},i_{7},i_{8},i_{9},i_{11},i_{12}
are not specified.

•

Find the next good tuple in the lexicographical order denoted by

(i_{4k-3},i_{4k-2},i_{4k-1},i_{4k}),

bound its contribution depending on the number of distinct indices
s
in the tuple. Repeat this process until no more good tuples can be found.

•

For all the remaining indices that have not been summed using (
160
), (
161
), or (
162
), let
k
be
the number of distinct indices in the remaining indices and bound their contribution by
d^{k}
.

Step 3: Applying the algorithm in 3 cases.

(a)
Case 1
: For the contribution in (
154
) with exactly 28 distinct indices in the sum, each is repeated exactly twice.
In this case, there are no good 2-tuples. To see that, suppose there exists one good 2-tuple
(i_{4k-3},i_{4k-2},i_{4k-1},i_{4k})
with
i_{4k-3}=i_{4k-1},i_{4k-2}=i_{4k}
and
i_{4k-3}\not=i_{4k-2}
. Then no other tuples will share the same index with
(i_{4k-3},i_{4k-2},i_{4k-1},i_{4k})
. By independence of entries in
{\boldsymbol{x}}
, this implies the contribution in (
157
) is zero. So below, we only need to consider sequences with good 3-tuples and 4-tuples. By applying the algorithm we described above, there are several cases:

•

Suppose all the good tuples are 3-tuples. We explain this case in more detail, and other cases below follow similarly.

Since each good 3-tuple has shared indices with at most 2 tuples, among 14 tuples, there are at least 5 good 3-tuples. We may assume the 5 good 3-tuples are

\displaystyle(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}),(i_{13},i_{14},i_{15},i_{16}),(i_{25},i_{26},i_{27},i_{28}),(i_{37},i_{38},i_{39},i_{40}),(i_{49},i_{50},i_{51},i_{52}).

(165)

Figure 7:
An example for the index sequences
(i_{1},\dots,i_{56})
with 5 good 3-tuples. An edge between an index from a good tuple and another index outside good tuples is drawn if the two indices are identical.

There are 15 distinct indices in (
165
) by definition. See Figure
7
for an example. Applying (
161
) to the 5 good 3-tuples, and (
163
) for the rest of the tuples, we can bound the contribution of this case to (
