) by

\displaystyle d^{12.5}\sum_{i_{6},i_{7},i_{8},i_{9},i_{11},i_{12}}\penalty\ \sum_{i_{18},i_{19},i_{20},i_{21},i_{23},i_{24}}\penalty\ \sum_{i_{30},i_{31},i_{32},i_{33},i_{35},i_{36}}\penalty\ \sum_{i_{42},i_{43},i_{44},i_{45},i_{47},i_{48}}\penalty\ \left(\sum_{i_{55},i_{56}}1\right)

(166)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle d^{12.5}\cdot d^{28-15}=d^{25.5},

(167)

where in the last inequality, we use the fact that there are at most
13
distinct indices that do not share any indices in (
165
), which gives the total contribution
O(d^{25.5})
.

•

Among 14 tuples, there are at least 3 good 4-tuples, which gives a contribution of
d^{9}
using (
160
). And there are
28-12=16
distinct indices remaining, which gives a contribution of
d^{16}
. In total, in this case, the contribution is
O(d^{25})
.

•

There are at least 2 good 4-tuples which give a contribution of
d^{6}
, and 1 good 3-tuples, which give a contribution of
d^{2.5}
. So the total contribution is
O(d^{25.5})
.

•

There are at least 1 good 4-tuples and 3 good 3-tuples. Similarly, the total contribution is
O(d^{3+7.5+(28-13)})=O(d^{25.5})
.

Therefore, from all the cases discussed above, the contribution for case (a) is bounded by
O(d^{25.5})
.

(b)
Case 2
: For the contribution of (
154
) with exactly 27 distinct indices in the sum. By counting the multiplicity, we must have one index appearing 4 times (since the third moment of
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}
is zero), and the rest of the 26 indices appear twice. In this case, to have a non-zero expectation, there are no good 2-tuples in (
154
). Otherwise, there will be at least two indices appearing 4 times.

Without loss of generality, we may assume the first tuple
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})
contains an index with multiplicity 4. There are at most 4 tuples containing this index, and we bound their contribution with (
163
). For the remaining 10 tuples, we apply the same argument as in Case (a). We have the following cases:

•

2 good 4-tuples. The total contribution is
O(d^{6+(27-8)})=O(d^{25})
.

•

1 good 4-tuple and 2 good 3-tuples, the total contribution is
O(d^{3+5+(27-10)})=O(d^{25})
.

•

4 good 3-tuples. The total contribution is
O(d^{10+(27-12)})=O(d^{25})
.

Therefore, all contribution for case (b) is
O(d^{25})
.

(c)
Case 3
: For the contribution of (
154
) with exactly 26 distinct indices in the sum. By counting the multiplicity, under the assumption that the 3rd and 5th moments of
{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}
is zero, there are two cases:

•

Case (c.1): one index appears 6 times, and the rest of the indices appear twice. To have a nonzero expectation, there are no good 2-tuples.
By a similar argument, assuming the index with multiplicity 6 is among the first tuple
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})
and is repeated in the first 6 tuples, we can bound their contribution using (
163
) and consider the remaining 8 tuples. For the remaining 8 tuples, we apply the same argument as in Case (a) in the following cases:

–

2 good 4 tuples: the contribution is
O(d^{6+26-8})=O(d^{24})
.

–

1 good 4-tuple and 1 good 3-tuple: the contribution is
O(d^{5.5+26-7})=O(d^{24.5})
.

–

3 good 3 tuples: the contribution is
O(d^{7.5+26-9})=O(d^{24.5})
.

The total contribution in this case is
O(d^{24.5})
.

•

Case (c.2): 2 indices appear 4 times. And the other 24 indices appear twice. In this case, we have at most one good 2-tuple.

Case (c.2.1): If there exists one good 2-tuple, then the 2 indices appearing 4 times must be in the same tuple to make a nonzero expectation. Without loss of generality, we assume
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})
is a good 2-tuple, and it shares common indices with the next 4 tuples. We may bound the contribution from the first 5 tuples using (
162
) and (
163
), which gives a contribution of
O(d^{1.5})
. There are 9 tuples left, and we have the following cases:

–

2 good 4-tuples, the total contribution is
O(d^{1.5+6+24-10})=O(d^{21.5})
.

–

1 good 4-tuples and 2 good 3-tuples, the total contribution is
O(d^{21.5})

–

3 good 3-tuples, the total contribution is
O(d^{1.5+7.5+(24-11)})=O(d^{22})
.

Case (c.2.2): Suppose there is no good 2-tuple.
Without loss of generality, we can assume
(i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4})
contains one index with multiplicity 4, with shared indices in the first 4 tuples. We can bound the contribution with (
163
). We can repeat this argument with the next 4 tuples: assume
(i_{17},i_{18},i_{19},i_{20})
contains one index with multiplicity 4 with shared indices in the next 3 tuples. Now we consider the remaining 6 tuples. There are several cases:
We could have

–

2 good 4-tuples, the total contribution is
O(d^{6+24-8})=O(d^{22})
.

–

1 good 4-tuple and 1 good 3-tuple, the total contribution is
O(d^{22.5})
.

–

2 good 3-tuples with a total contribution
O(d^{5+24-6})=O(d^{23})
.

Combining cases (a), (b), and (c), (
148
) holds. By Markov’s inequality and a union bound over
[n]
, (
149
) follows.
∎

E.1.2
Deterministic equivalence of functions of the kernel

Next, we prove the following limits for the sample covariance matrix
\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)\top}\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)}
, which will be utilized in the analysis of generalization error in Section
E.2
.

Lemma 31
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
2
, as
n\to\infty
, we have in probability,

\displaystyle a_{2}\operatorname{Tr}\big((a_{2}\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)\top}\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)}+(a+\lambda){\mathbf{I}})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}\big)\to\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(0)\lambda_{*}}{4\alpha(a_{*}+\lambda)}-1,

(168)

\displaystyle a_{2}(a+\lambda)\operatorname{Tr}\big((a_{2}\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)\top}\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)}+(a+\lambda){\mathbf{I}})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}\big)\to\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{f^{\prime\prime}(0)\lambda_{*}}{4\alpha(a_{*}+\lambda)}-\frac{1}{1-\alpha\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x^{2}}{(x+\lambda_{*})^{2}}d\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x)},

(169)

\displaystyle\frac{2}{d^{2}}\operatorname{Tr}\big((a+\lambda){\mathbf{I}}+a_{2}\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)\top}\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)}\big)^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}\big)\to\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{\mathcal{B}(\lambda_{*})}{(a_{*}+\lambda)^{2}},

(170)

where
\lambda_{*}>0
is defined by equation (
28
) and
\mathcal{B}(\lambda_{*})
is defined by (
30
).

Proof.

Let us define
z_{n}:=\frac{2d^{2}(a+\lambda)}{nf^{\prime\prime}(0)}>0
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Notice that

\displaystyle a_{2}\operatorname{Tr}\big((a_{2}\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)\top}\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)}+(a+\lambda){\mathbf{I}})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}\big)=\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr}\big((\frac{1}{n}\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)\top}\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)}+z_{n}{\mathbf{I}})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}\big)

(171)

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr}\big((\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)\top}+z_{n}{\mathbf{I}})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}\big)

(172)

where
\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)}
is defined by (
90
) for
i\in[n]
. Next, we follow the proof of Lemma 2.2 in
(
Ledoit and Péché, 2011
)
to complete the proof (see also
(
Wang et al., 2024
, Theorem 10)
).
For any fixed
z>0
, we define
{\boldsymbol{R}}(z):=(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)\top}+z{\mathbf{I}})^{-1}
and
{\boldsymbol{R}}^{(k)}(z):=(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\in[n\setminus\{k\}]}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)\top}+z{\mathbf{I}})^{-1}
for any
k\in[n]
. Then, by the Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury formula, we have

\frac{1}{n}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)\top}{\boldsymbol{R}}(z)\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)}=1-\frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{n}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)\top}{\boldsymbol{R}}^{(i)}(z)\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)}}.

(173)

Notice that

{\boldsymbol{R}}(z)\Big(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)\top}+z{\mathbf{I}}\Big)={\mathbf{I}}
.
Taking trace and applying (
173
), we obtain

\displaystyle 1+\frac{z}{n}\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{R}}(z)=\frac{\binom{d+1}{2}}{n}+\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{n}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)\top}{\boldsymbol{R}}^{(i)}(z)\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)}}.

(174)

Notice that
\left\|{\boldsymbol{R}}^{(i)}(z)\right\|\leq 1/z
for all
i\in[n]
. Then, applying (
148
) in Lemma
30
with matrix
{\boldsymbol{A}}={\boldsymbol{R}}^{(i)}(z)
for
i\in[n]
we have, by a union bound over
i\in[n]
,

\max_{i\in[n]}\left|\frac{1}{n}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)\top}{\boldsymbol{R}}^{(i)}(z)\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)}-\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{R}}^{(i)}(z){\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)})\right|=O(n^{-\frac{1}{60}})

(175)

with probability at least
1-O(d^{-1/5})
, for any fixed
z>0
. Additionally, by the Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury formula, we also have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\Big|\operatorname{Tr}(({\boldsymbol{R}}^{(i)}(z)-{\boldsymbol{R}}(z)){\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)})\Big|\leq\frac{1}{n}\left|\frac{\frac{1}{n}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)\top}{\boldsymbol{R}}^{(i)}(z){\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}{\boldsymbol{R}}^{(i)}(z)\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)}}{1+\frac{1}{n}\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)\top}{\boldsymbol{R}}^{(i)}(z)\overline{{\boldsymbol{x}}}_{i}^{(2)}}\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{n},

(176)

where we applied the assumption of
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}
,
\|{\boldsymbol{R}}^{(i)}(z)\|\leq 1/z
and positive definiteness of
{\boldsymbol{R}}^{(i)}(z)
. Then, from (
174
), (
175
), and (
176
), we have with probability at least
1-O(d^{-1/5})
,

\displaystyle 1+\frac{z}{n}\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{R}}(z)=\frac{\binom{d+1}{2}}{n}+\frac{1}{1+\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{R}}(z){\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}+o(1),

(177)

where we used the fact that
1+\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{R}}^{(i)}(z){\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)})>1
,
for any
z>0
. Thus, applying Theorem
2
, we can claim that for any
z>0
,

\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{R}}(z){\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}\to\frac{1}{z\alpha m(-z)+1-\alpha}-1=\frac{1}{z\widetilde{m}(-z)}-1,

(178)

in probability as
n\to\infty
, where
m(-z)
and
\widetilde{m}(-z)
are defined in Definition
11
with
\nu=\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}
in Assumption
2.1
. Consider
z:=\frac{4\alpha(a_{*}+\lambda)}{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}>0
. Then, the fixed point equation (
28
) defines
\lambda_{*}=\frac{1}{\widetilde{m}(-z)}>0
. Furthermore, notice that
z_{n}\to z=\frac{4\alpha(a_{*}+\lambda)}{f^{\prime\prime}(0)}
as
n\to\infty
. Thus,

\frac{1}{n}\big|\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{R}}(z){\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}-\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{R}}(z_{n}){\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}\big|\lesssim|z-z_{n}|\to 0
.
This completes the proof of the first part of this lemma.

For the second part of this lemma, we follow the proof in Lemma 7.4 of

(
Dobriban and Wager, 2018
)
. Notice that (
178
) holds for any
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
\operatorname{Re}(z)>0
and
\frac{1}{n}|\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{R}}(z){\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}|\lesssim 1
. Based on Lemma 2.14 in
(
Bai and Silverstein, 2010
)
, we can obtain that

\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{R}}(z)^{2}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}\to\frac{\widetilde{m}(-z)-z\widetilde{m}^{\prime}(-z)}{z^{2}\widetilde{m}^{2}(-z)},

(179)

in probability, for any
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
\operatorname{Re}(z)>0
. From (
34
), we know that

\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{m}^{\prime}(-z)}{\widetilde{m}^{2}(-z)}=\frac{1}{1-\alpha\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x^{2}}{(x+\lambda_{*})^{2}}d\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x)}.

(180)

Then, because of

a_{2}(a+\lambda)\operatorname{Tr}\big((a_{2}\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)\top}\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)}+(a+\lambda){\mathbf{I}})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}\big)=z_{n}\cdot\frac{1}{n}\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{R}}(z_{n})^{2}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)},

we can similarly derive the second part of the results. Lastly, since

\displaystyle\frac{2}{d^{2}}\operatorname{Tr}\big(\big((a+\lambda){\mathbf{I}}+a_{2}\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)\top}\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)}\big)^{-2}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}\big)

(181)

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{4}{f^{\prime\prime}(0)(a_{*}+\lambda)}\left(\lambda_{*}/z-\frac{1}{1-\alpha\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{x^{2}}{(x+\lambda_{*})^{2}}d\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x)}\right)

(182)

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{4}{f^{\prime\prime}(0)(a_{*}+\lambda)}\frac{\alpha\lambda_{*}^{2}\int\frac{x}{(x+\lambda_{*})^{2}}d\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x)}{z(1-\alpha\int\frac{x^{2}}{(x+\lambda_{*})^{2}}d\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x))}

(183)

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda_{*}^{2}}{(a_{*}+\lambda)^{2}}\frac{\int\frac{x}{(x+\lambda_{*})^{2}}d\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x)}{(1-\alpha\int\frac{x^{2}}{(x+\lambda_{*})^{2}}d\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x))},

(184)

we can apply (
179
) and (
180
) to conclude the final result of this lemma.
Here we also use the fixed point equation (
28
) of
\lambda_{*}
:

1-\frac{z}{\lambda_{*}}=\alpha\int\frac{x}{x+\lambda_{*}}d\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x)=\alpha\int\frac{x^{2}+\lambda_{*}x}{(x+\lambda_{*})^{2}}d\mu_{{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}}(x).

(185)

∎

E.1.3
Spectral norm concentrations

Next, we provide spectral norm bounds on
{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}
and
({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}
below.

Lemma 32
.

Under Assumptions
2.1
,
2.1
, and
2.1
, with a probability of at least
1-O(d^{-\frac{1}{48}})
, we have

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}\|\lesssim\|{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim d^{2+\frac{1}{24}},

(186)

\displaystyle\|({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}\|\lesssim\|({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim d^{3},

(187)

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}\|

\displaystyle\lesssim d^{1+\frac{1}{12}}.

(188)

Proof.

We first show (
186
) with Latala’s Theorem
(
Latala, 2005
)
. We can write
{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\top}
, where
{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{\top}=[{\boldsymbol{z}}_{1},\dots,{\boldsymbol{z}}_{n}]
is a
d\times n
random matrix with independent entries and each entry of
{\boldsymbol{Z}}
has zero mean and finite fourth moments. By
(
Latala, 2005
, Theorem 2)
, we have

\mathbb{E}\|{\boldsymbol{Z}}\|\lesssim\sqrt{n}+\sqrt{d}+(nd)^{1/4}\lesssim d
.
Then by Markov’s inequality, with probability at least
1-O(d^{-\frac{1}{48}})
,

\|{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}\|\lesssim\|{\boldsymbol{Z}}\|^{2}\lesssim d^{2+\frac{1}{24}}
.

Next, we show (
187
). Since
({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}={{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}{{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}
, it suffices to consider
{{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{(2)}{{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{(2)}}^{\top}
, which is a sum of
n
i.i.d. rank-1 matrices. We will use matrix Bernstein’s inequality
(
Vershynin, 2018
, Theorem 5.4.1)
to prove (
187
). Consider truncated vectors
{{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}}:={\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{(2)}\mathbf{1}\{\|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{(2)}\|\leq Bd\}
for a parameter
B=n^{\frac{1}{44}}
. Let
{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}
be the truncated version of
{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}
. We have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}\not={\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{i\in[n]}\|{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{(2)}\|>Bd\right)\leq\frac{n\mathbb{E}\|{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)}\|^{45}}{(Bd)^{45}}\lesssim\frac{n}{B^{45}}\lesssim n^{-\frac{1}{45}}.

(189)

On the other hand, almost surely,

\left\|{{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}}{{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}}^{\top}-\mathbb{E}{{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}}{{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}}^{\top}\right\|\lesssim(Bd)^{2}
,
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left({{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}}{{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}}^{\top}-\mathbb{E}{{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}}{{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}}^{\top}\right)^{2}\preccurlyeq\mathbb{E}\left[\|{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}\|^{2}{{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}}{{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}}^{\top}\right]\preccurlyeq(Bd)^{2}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}\leq C(Bd)^{2}{\mathbf{I}}

(190)

for some constant
C>0
due to Assumption
2.1
. By matrix Bernstein’s inequality
(
Vershynin, 2018
, Theorem 5.4.1)
, we have with probability at least
1-d^{2}\exp(-\frac{5}{66}d)
,

\displaystyle\left\|{{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}\right\|\lesssim d^{2+\frac{1}{6}}.

(191)

We also have

\mathbb{E}{{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}\lesssim n\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)}{{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)}}^{\top}\leq Cd^{3}{\mathbf{I}}
,
where we use the definition of
{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)}
from (
14
).
Together with (
189
), we have with probability at least
1-O(d^{-\frac{2}{45}})
,

\|({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}\|\lesssim d^{3}
.

For (
188
), we have

\displaystyle\|{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}\|\leq\|{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}-{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}\|+\|{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}\|+\|\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}\|.

(192)

From (
189
), with probability
1-O(n^{-1/45})
, the first term in (
192
) is zero. For the second term in (
192
), we consider
\|{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}\|^{2}=\|({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)})({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)})^{\top}\|
,
where

\displaystyle({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)})({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)})^{\top}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}({\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)})({\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)})^{\top},

(193)

and apply the matrix Bernstein’s inequality.
We have almost surely,
\|({\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)})({\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)})^{\top}\|\leq 4(Bd)^{2}
.
And for some constant
C>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left(({\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)})({\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)})^{\top}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left\|{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}\right\|^{2}({\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)})({\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)})^{\top}

(194)

\displaystyle\leq 4(Bd)^{2}\mathbb{E}({\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)})({\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}^{(2)})^{\top}

(195)

\displaystyle\leq 4(Bd)^{2}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}\lesssim C(Bd)^{2}{\mathbf{I}}.

(196)

With matrix Bernstein’s inequality
(
Vershynin, 2018
, Theorem 5.4.1)
, we have with probability at least
1-d^{2}\exp(-\frac{5}{66}d)
,

\|({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)})({\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)})^{\top}\|\lesssim d^{2+\frac{1}{6}}
.
Hence with probability
1-O(d^{-\frac{2}{45}})
, from (
192
),

\|{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}\|\lesssim d^{1+\frac{1}{12}}+\|\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}\|
. Since each column of
{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}
has the same distribution,
\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}
is of rank 1. We obtain

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}\|

\displaystyle=\|\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{Z}}^{(2)}\|_{\mathsf{F}}=\sqrt{n}\mathbb{E}[\|{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)}\|\mathbf{1}\{\|{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)}\|\geq Bd\}]

(197)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{n}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}[\|{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)}\|^{2}]}\sqrt{\mathbb{P}(\|{\boldsymbol{x}}^{(2)}\|\geq Bd)}\lesssim\sqrt{nd^{2}B^{-45}}\lesssim\sqrt{d^{2}n^{-\frac{1}{44}}}=d^{1-\frac{1}{44}},

(198)

where in the second inequality we use (
189
). Therefore we obtain with probability
1-O(d^{-\frac{2}{45}})
,

\|{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}\|\lesssim d^{1+\frac{1}{12}}

as desired. This finishes the proof.
∎

E.1.4
Kernel function expansion

Recall
{\boldsymbol{x}}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{z}}
and
\boldsymbol{w}_{i}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}
for
i\in[n]
and
{\boldsymbol{z}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,{\mathbf{I}})
. Let
t_{i}={\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}=\|\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\|^{2}
and
{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i}=\frac{\boldsymbol{w}_{i}}{\|\boldsymbol{w}_{i}\|}
. Then

\displaystyle\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle=\sqrt{t_{i}}\langle{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{z}}\rangle,

(199)

and for
j=0,\dots,8
and
i\in[n]
, define

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{T}_{i}^{(j)}:=t_{i}^{j/2}\sqrt{j!}\cdot h_{j}\left(\langle{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{z}}\rangle\right),

(200)

where
h_{j}
is the
j
-th normalized Hermite polynomial defined in Definition
12
.

Lemma 33
.

Under Assumption
2.3.2
, we have for any
i,j\in[n]
,

\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i}^{(k)}\boldsymbol{T}_{j}^{(\ell)}]=0
if
k\neq\ell
and
k+\ell\leq 15
, and for all
k=0,1,\ldots,8
,

\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i}^{(k)}\boldsymbol{T}_{j}^{(k)}]=k!\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{j}\rangle^{k}
, where
{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}:={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}
.

Proof.

Since the calculation of
\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i}^{(k)}\boldsymbol{T}_{j}^{(\ell)}]
involves only the first 16th moments of
{\boldsymbol{z}}
for
k+\ell\leq 15
, by the orthogonality property of
h_{j}
in Lemma
13
and assumption
2.3.2
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i}^{(k)}\boldsymbol{T}_{j}^{(\ell)}]=\penalty\

\displaystyle t_{i}^{k/2}t_{j}^{\ell/2}\sqrt{k!\ell!}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{z}}}[h_{k}\left(\langle{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{z}}\rangle\right)h_{\ell}(\langle{\boldsymbol{u}}_{j},{\boldsymbol{z}}\rangle)]

(201)

\displaystyle=\penalty\

\displaystyle\delta_{k,\ell}\cdot k!t_{i}^{k/2}t_{j}^{k/2}\langle{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{u}}_{j}\rangle^{k}=\delta_{k,\ell}\cdot k!\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{j}\rangle^{k}.

(202)

Hence,
\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i}^{(k)}\boldsymbol{T}_{j}^{(\ell)}]=0
if
k\not=\ell
. This finishes the proof.
∎

For any
i\in[n]
, let us apply the Taylor expansion of
f
as in (
39
) to get

\displaystyle K({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}})=\sum_{k=0}^{8}\frac{f^{(k)}(0)}{k!d^{k}}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle^{k}+\frac{f^{(9)}(\zeta_{i})}{9!d^{9}}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle^{9},

(203)

where
\zeta_{i}
is between
0
and
\frac{1}{d}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle
.

Recall (
199
), we have

\sum_{k=0}^{8}\frac{f^{(k)}(0)}{k!d^{k}}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle^{k}=\sum_{k=0}^{8}\frac{f^{(k)}(0)}{k!d^{k}}t_{i}^{k/2}\langle{\boldsymbol{u}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{z}}\rangle^{k},

where
t_{i}:={\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}
for
i\in[n]
.
With Lemma
33
and (
200
), we can rewrite
K({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}})
as

\displaystyle K({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}})=\penalty\

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{8}b_{k,i}{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i}^{(k)}+\frac{f^{(9)}(\zeta_{i})}{9!d^{9}}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle^{9}.

(204)

By orthogonality of the normalized Hermite polynomials, we have

\displaystyle b_{0,i}=\penalty\

\displaystyle f(0)+t_{i}\cdot\frac{f^{(2)}(0)}{2!d^{2}}+3t_{i}^{2}\cdot\frac{f^{(4)}(0)}{4!d^{4}}+15t_{i}^{3}\cdot\frac{f^{(6)}(0)}{6!d^{6}},

(205)

\displaystyle b_{1,i}=\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{f^{(1)}(0)}{d}+3t_{i}\cdot\frac{f^{(3)}(0)}{3!d^{3}}+15t_{i}^{2}\cdot\frac{f^{(5)}(0)}{5!d^{5}}+105t_{i}^{3}\cdot\frac{f^{(7)}(0)}{7!d^{7}},

(206)

\displaystyle b_{2,i}=\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{f^{(2)}(0)}{2!d^{2}}+6t_{i}\cdot\frac{f^{(4)}(0)}{4!d^{4}}+45t_{i}^{2}\frac{f^{(6)}(0)}{6!d^{6}},

(207)

\displaystyle b_{3,i}=\penalty\

\displaystyle\frac{f^{(3)}(0)}{3!d^{3}}+10t_{i}\cdot\frac{f^{(5)}(0)}{5!d^{5}}+105t_{i}^{2}\cdot\frac{f^{(7)}(0)}{k!d^{7}}.

(208)

In general,
for
0\leq k\leq 8
,
b_{k,i}t_{i}^{k/2}\sqrt{k!}=\sum_{s=k}^{8}t_{i}^{s/2}\frac{f^{(s)}(0)}{s!d^{s}}\mathbb{E}_{g\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)}[g^{s}h_{k}(g)].

Therefore,

\displaystyle|b_{k,i}|\lesssim\sum_{s=k}^{8}d^{-s}t_{i}^{(s-k)/2}.

(209)

Utilizing (
56
), we can easily check that

|t_{i}-\operatorname{Tr}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}|\lesssim d^{\frac{1}{2}+\frac{1}{30}},

(210)

uniformly for all
i\in[n]
with probability at least
1-d^{-1}
. Thus,
0\leq t_{i}\lesssim d
. Therefore, from (
209
), for
k=0,1,\ldots,8
and all
i\in[n]
, with probability at least
1-d^{-1}
,

|b_{k,i}|\lesssim d^{-k}.

(211)

Lemma 34
.

Let us denote that

\displaystyle\widetilde{b}_{0,i}:=\penalty\ f(0)+t_{i}\cdot\frac{f^{(2)}(0)}{2!d^{2}},\quad\widetilde{b}_{1,i}:=\penalty\ \frac{f^{(1)}(0)}{d}+3t_{i}\cdot\frac{f^{(3)}(0)}{3!d^{3}}

(212)

for any
i\in[n]
. Then, under Assumption
2.1
, we have

\displaystyle\max_{i\in[n]}|\widetilde{b}_{0,i}-b_{0,i}|\lesssim\penalty\ d^{-2},\quad\max_{i\in[n]}|\widetilde{b}_{1,i}-b_{1,i}|\lesssim\penalty\ d^{-3},\quad\max_{i\in[n]}|a_{2}-b_{2,i}|\lesssim\penalty\ d^{-3.4}

(213)

with probability at least
1-d^{-1}
, where
a_{2}
is defined in (
11
).

Proof.

The first two bounds are directly from (
210
). Recall the definition of
a_{2}
in (
11
). Then for the last bound, we have

\displaystyle b_{2,i}-a_{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{f^{(4)}(0)}{4d^{4}}(t_{i}-\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2}))+45t_{i}^{2}\frac{f^{(6)}(0)}{6!d^{6}}.

(214)

Applying (
210
), we can derive that

|b_{2,i}-a_{2}|\lesssim\frac{1}{d^{4}}|t_{i}-\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})|+\frac{1}{d^{6}}|t_{i}^{2}|\lesssim d^{-3.4}

uniformly for all
i\in[n]
with probability at least
1-d^{-1}
.
∎

E.1.5
Approximation of product of kernel functions

Denote
{\boldsymbol{M}}:=\penalty\ \mathbb{E}[K({\boldsymbol{X}},{\boldsymbol{x}})K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{X}})|{\boldsymbol{X}}],{\boldsymbol{v}}:=\penalty\ \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{x}}[f_{*}({\boldsymbol{x}})K({\boldsymbol{X}},{\boldsymbol{x}})]
,
where

K({\boldsymbol{X}},{\boldsymbol{x}})=[K({\boldsymbol{x}}_{1},{\boldsymbol{x}}),\dots,K({\boldsymbol{x}}_{n},{\boldsymbol{x}})]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}

and
\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{x}}[\cdot]
denotes the expectation only with respect to
{\boldsymbol{x}}
. Notice that for any
i,j\in[n]
,

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{M}}_{ij}=\penalty\ (\mathbb{E}[K({\boldsymbol{X}},{\boldsymbol{x}})K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{X}})])_{ij}=\mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{x}}[K({\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}})K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j})],\quad{\boldsymbol{v}}_{i}=\penalty\ \mathbb{E}_{\boldsymbol{x}}[K({\boldsymbol{x}},{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i})f_{*}({\boldsymbol{x}})].

We define

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{b}}_{0}=\penalty\

\displaystyle(b_{0,1},\dots,b_{0,n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad{\boldsymbol{b}}_{1}=(b_{1,1},\dots,b_{1,n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

(215)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\boldsymbol{b}}_{0}=\penalty\

\displaystyle(\widetilde{b}_{0,1},\dots,\widetilde{b}_{0,n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},\quad\widetilde{\boldsymbol{b}}_{1}=(\widetilde{b}_{1,1},\dots,\widetilde{b}_{1,n})^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n},

(216)

where
b_{0,i},b_{1,i},\widetilde{b}_{0,i}
, and
\widetilde{b}_{1,i}
are defined in (
205
), (
206
), (
216
), and (
212
), respectively. Denote

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{M}}^{(2)}:={\boldsymbol{b}}_{0}{\boldsymbol{b}}_{0}^{\top}+\text{diag}({\boldsymbol{b}}_{1}){\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}\text{diag}({\boldsymbol{b}}_{1})+2a^{2}_{2}{\boldsymbol{M}}^{(2)}_{0},\quad{\boldsymbol{M}}^{(2)}_{0}:=({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}.

(217)

In the following, we first provide an approximation of
{\boldsymbol{M}}
in terms of
{\boldsymbol{M}}^{(2)}
.

Lemma 35
.

Under the same assumptions as Theorem
1
, we have that

\|{{\boldsymbol{M}}-{\boldsymbol{M}}^{(2)}}\|\lesssim\penalty\ \frac{1}{d^{9/4}}
,

with probability
1-O(d^{-1/48})
.

Proof.

For
i,j\in[n]
, we can apply the orthogonality property in Lemma
33
to get

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{M}}_{ij}=\penalty\

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{8}b_{k,i}b_{k,j}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i}^{(k)}\boldsymbol{T}_{j}^{(k)}]+\sum_{k=0}^{8}\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}\Big[b_{k,i}\boldsymbol{T}_{i}^{(k)}\frac{f^{(9)}(\zeta_{j})}{9!d^{9}}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j},{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle^{9}\Big]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{8}\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}\big[b_{k,j}\boldsymbol{T}_{j}^{(k)}\frac{f^{(9)}(\zeta_{i})}{9!d^{9}}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle^{9}\Big]+\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}\Big[\frac{f^{(9)}(\zeta_{i})f^{(9)}(\zeta_{j})}{(9!)^{2}d^{18}}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i},{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle^{9}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j},{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle^{9}\Big]

\displaystyle=:\penalty\

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{L}}_{i,j}+\boldsymbol{V}_{i,j}^{(1)}+\boldsymbol{V}_{i,j}^{(2)}+\boldsymbol{V}_{i,j}^{(3)}.

Recall that
{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{1/2}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}
for all
i\in[n]
. By the assumption that
f^{(9)}(x)
is uniformly bounded in Assumption
2.3.2
, we have from (
211
), with probability
1-O(d^{-1})
,

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{V}_{i,j}^{(1)}|\lesssim\penalty\

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{8}\frac{1}{d^{9+k}}\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}[|{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i}^{(k)}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j},{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle^{9}|]\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{8}\frac{1}{d^{9+k}}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}|{\boldsymbol{T}}_{i}^{(k)}|^{2}}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}\langle{\boldsymbol{x}}_{j},{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle^{18}}

(218)

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{k=0}^{8}\frac{1}{d^{k+9}}\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\|^{k}\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{j}\|^{9},

(219)

where in the last inequality, we use Lemma
33
and Lemma
16
under the Gaussian moment matching condition in Assumption
2.3.2
.
Similarly,

\displaystyle|\boldsymbol{V}_{i,j}^{(2)}|\lesssim\penalty\ \sum_{k=0}^{8}\frac{1}{d^{k+9}}\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{j}\|^{k}\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\|^{9},\quad|\boldsymbol{V}_{i,j}^{(3)}|\lesssim\penalty\ \frac{1}{d^{18}}\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\|^{9}\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{j}\|^{9}.

(220)

Notice that the leading order
|\boldsymbol{V}_{i,j}^{(\ell)}|\lesssim\frac{1}{d^{8}}\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\|^{8}
for
\ell=1,2
. Recall (
55
), i.e.,
\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2s}\right]=\mathbb{E}[\|{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{z}}_{i}\|^{2s}]\lesssim d^{s}
for any
1\leq s\leq 45
. Thus, Markov’s inequality implies that

\mathbb{P}(|\boldsymbol{V}_{i,j}^{(\ell)}|>t)\leq\frac{1}{(d^{4.5}t)^{s}}

for all
i,j\in[n]
and
\ell=1,2
. Then taking
t=d^{-17/4}
and
s=18
, then taking union bounds for all
i,j\in[n]
, we can derive that
\left\|\boldsymbol{V}^{(\ell)}\right\|\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{V}^{(\ell)}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\lesssim d^{-9/4}
with probability at least
1-cd^{-1/2}
for some constant
c>0
and
\ell=1,2
. Similarly, we can verify the same bound holds for
\ell=3
.

Let us further define matrices
\boldsymbol{L}^{(k)}
whose
(i,j)
entry is given by

\boldsymbol{L}^{(k)}_{i,j}:=b_{k,i}b_{k,j}\cdot\mathbb{E}_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}[\boldsymbol{T}_{i}^{(k)}\boldsymbol{T}_{j}^{(k)}]=k!b_{k,i}b_{k,j}\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{j},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\rangle^{k}

for
i,j\in[n]
and
0\leq k\leq 8
, where we applied Lemma
33
.
We next employ (
55
) and (
56
) to deduce that

\left\|\boldsymbol{L}^{(k)}\right\|\lesssim\frac{1}{d^{9/4}}
,
for
3\leq k\leq 8
, with probability at least
1-O(d^{-1/2})
.
Let us extract the diagonal matrix of
\boldsymbol{L}^{(k)}
by denoting
\boldsymbol{L}^{(k)}_{\text{diag}}
. Set
\boldsymbol{L}^{(k)}_{\text{off}}:=\boldsymbol{L}^{(k)}-\boldsymbol{L}^{(k)}_{\text{diag}}
. Then, we bound the operator norms of
\boldsymbol{L}^{(k)}_{\text{off}}
and
\boldsymbol{L}^{(k)}_{\text{diag}}
separately. First,

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{L}^{(k)}_{\text{off}}\right\|\leq\left\|\boldsymbol{L}^{(k)}_{\text{off}}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\lesssim\frac{n}{d^{2k}}\max_{i\neq j}\langle{\boldsymbol{w}}_{j},{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\rangle^{k}\lesssim\frac{1}{d^{2.5}},

(221)

with probability at least
1-O(d^{-1/2})
, for
3\leq k\leq 8
. Next, for the diagonal part, we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{L}^{(k)}_{\text{diag}}\right\|\lesssim\frac{1}{d^{2k}}\max_{i\in[n]}\left\|{\boldsymbol{w}}_{i}\right\|^{2k}\lesssim\frac{1}{d^{3}}
,
with probability at least
1-O(d^{-1/2})
, for
3\leq k\leq 8
.

Lastly, let us denote that
{\boldsymbol{b}}_{2}=[b_{2,1},\ldots,b_{2,n}]^{\top}
. Hence,

\boldsymbol{L}^{(2)}=2\text{diag}({\boldsymbol{b}}_{2})({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}\text{diag}({\boldsymbol{b}}_{2}).

Lemma
34
proves that
|b_{2,i}-a_{2}|\lesssim 1/d^{3.4}
and
|b_{2,i}|\lesssim 1/d^{2}
with probability
1-d^{-1}
for all
i\in[n]
. Moreover,
|a_{2}|\lesssim 1/d^{2}
. Then, by Lemma
32
, with probability at least
1-O(d^{-\frac{1}{48}})
,

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{L}^{(2)}-2a^{2}_{2}{\boldsymbol{M}}_{0}^{(2)}\right\|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\left\|\text{diag}({\boldsymbol{b}}_{2})({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}\right\|+a_{2}\left\|({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}\right\|\right)\max_{i\in[n]}|b_{2,i}-a_{2}|\lesssim d^{-{2.4}}.

(222)

Then, we complete the proof of the approximation on
{\boldsymbol{M}}
by
{\boldsymbol{M}}^{(2)}
.
∎

Lemma 36
.

With Assumption
2.2
, we have

{\boldsymbol{M}}_{0}^{(2)}=\frac{1}{2}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)\top}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{d}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{kk}^{2}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{k}\boldsymbol{\nu}_{k}^{\top},

(223)

where
\boldsymbol{\nu}_{k}:=[{\boldsymbol{x}}_{1}(k)^{2},\ldots,{\boldsymbol{x}}_{n}(k)^{2}]^{\top}
for
k\in[d]
and
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}
is defined by (
17
). Moreover, under the Assumption
2.3.2
, we have

\left\|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{k}\right\|\lesssim d^{1+\frac{1}{22}}
for all
k\in[d]
, with probability at least
1-d^{-1}
.

Proof.

By the definition of
{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{(2)}
in (
17
), we can easily check (
223
). Notice that
\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\nu}_{k}]={\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}_{kk}\mathbf{1}
and
\left\|\mathbb{E}[\boldsymbol{\nu}_{k}]\right\|\lesssim\sqrt{n}
. By the Assumptions
2.3.2
and
2.2
, we know that

\mathbb{E}[\left\|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{k}\right\|^{2s}]=\mathbb{E}[(\sum_{i=1}^{n}{{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}(k)^{4}})^{s}]\lesssim d^{2s}
, for
0\leq 4s\leq 90
. Then, we can conclude the final bound of this lemma by taking
s=22
and applying Markov inequality for
\left\|\boldsymbol{\nu}_{k}\right\|
.
∎

E.1.6
Resolvent calculations

Lemma 37
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}})^{-2}\mathbf{1}\lesssim d^{-\frac{23}{24}},\quad\mathbf{1}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}})^{-1}\mathbf{1}\lesssim 1,\quad\big|1-b_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}\mathbf{1}\big|\lesssim\penalty\ d^{-\frac{23}{24}}

(224)

with probability at least
1-O(d^{-1/48})
, where
b_{0}:=f(0)
.

Proof.

Denote
\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}:=(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}})^{-1}
. From Theorem
1
, there exists a matrix
\boldsymbol{K}_{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
such that with probability at least
1-O(d^{-1/2})
,

\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}=\boldsymbol{K}_{*}+a_{0}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top},\quad\left\|\boldsymbol{K}_{*}-a_{1}{\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top}+a_{2}({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}+(a+\lambda){\mathbf{I}}_{n}\right\|\lesssim d^{-\frac{1}{12}}.

Thus, by Assumption
2.3.1
and Lemma
32
,
c{\mathbf{I}}\preccurlyeq\boldsymbol{K}_{*}\preccurlyeq Cd^{1+\frac{1}{24}}{\mathbf{I}}
, for some constants
c,C>0
with probability
1-O(d^{-1/48})
. By the Sherman-Morrison-Woodbury formula, we have

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}=\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-1}-a_{0}\frac{\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-1}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-1}}{1+a_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-1}\mathbf{1}}.

(225)

Therefore, we can obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-2}

(226)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-2}+\frac{(a_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-1}\mathbf{1})(a_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-2}\mathbf{1})}{(1+a_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-1}\mathbf{1})^{2}}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-1}-\frac{a_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-2}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-1}}{1+a_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-1}\mathbf{1}}-\frac{a_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-1}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-2}}{1+a_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-1}\mathbf{1}}

(227)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{a_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-2}\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-1}}{(1+a_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-1}\mathbf{1})^{2}}+\frac{\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-2}}{1+a_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-1}\mathbf{1}}.

(228)

Thus, we have

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{n}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-2}\mathbf{1}_{n}=\frac{\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-2}\mathbf{1}}{(1+a_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-1}\mathbf{1})^{2}}\leq\frac{1}{ca_{0}^{2}}\frac{\mathbf{1}_{n}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{n}}{(\mathbf{1}_{n}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{n})^{2}}\lesssim\frac{d^{1+1/24}}{\|\mathbf{1}_{n}\|^{2}}\lesssim\frac{1}{d^{23/24}}.

(229)

with probability at least
1-O(d^{-1/48})
. The second bound in this lemma comes directly from (
225
) since

a_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}})^{-1}\mathbf{1}=\frac{a_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-1}\mathbf{1}}{1+a_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-1}\mathbf{1}}\leq 1
.
Lastly, (
225
) implies that

1-a_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}\mathbf{1}=\frac{1}{1+a_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{-1}\mathbf{1}}
.
The same bound as (
229
) can be employed here to get

|1-a_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}\mathbf{1}|\lesssim d^{-\frac{23}{24}}
,
with probability at least
1-O(d^{-1/48})
. Hence,

|1-b_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}\mathbf{1}|\leq|1-a_{0}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}\mathbf{1}|+|a_{0}-b_{0}|\cdot\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}\mathbf{1}\lesssim d^{-\frac{23}{24}},

with probability at least
1-O(d^{-1/48})
.
∎

Let us denote

\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\top}:=[t_{1},t_{2},\ldots,t_{n}],

(230)

where
t_{i}={\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}^{\top}{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}{\boldsymbol{x}}_{i}
, for
i\in[n]
. Recall
\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)}={\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}]
and notice that

\displaystyle({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}=\penalty\

\displaystyle\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)}\overline{{\boldsymbol{X}}}^{(2)\top}+\left({\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}]^{\top}-\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}]\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}]^{\top}+\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}]{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)\top}\right),

(231)

where

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}]^{\top}=\penalty\ \boldsymbol{\mu}\mathbf{1}^{\top},\quad\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}]^{\top}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}=\penalty\ \mathbf{1}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\top},\quad\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}]\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}]^{\top}=\penalty\ \operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})\cdot\mathbf{1}\mathbf{1}^{\top}.

(232)

Thus, we define
{\boldsymbol{U}}:=[\mathbf{1},\boldsymbol{\mu}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times 2}
. Then,

a_{2}({\boldsymbol{X}}{\boldsymbol{X}}^{\top})^{\odot 2}=\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{(2)}+a_{2}{\boldsymbol{U}}\begin{pmatrix}-\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})&1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}{\boldsymbol{U}}^{\top}

(233)

where

\boldsymbol{K}_{*}^{(2)}:=a_{2}({\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}])({\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}-\mathbb{E}[{\boldsymbol{X}}^{(2)}])^{\top}.

(234)

Lemma 38
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1
and Assumption
2.2
, with probability at least
1-O(d^{-1/2})
,

\frac{1}{d^{4}}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}\lesssim d^{-0.8},

where
\boldsymbol{\mu}
is defined by (
230
).
As a corollary, we also have

\frac{1}{d^{2}}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}\lesssim d^{-0.4}
.

Proof.

Let
\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}:=\mathbb{E}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})\mathbf{1}
. Due to (
210
), we can conclude that

\displaystyle\left\|\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}\right\|\lesssim d^{1.6},

(235)

with probability at least
1-O(d^{-1})
.
Thus,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}=\penalty\

\displaystyle(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0})^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0})+\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}+2(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0})^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}.

(236)

Here, we know that
\frac{1}{d^{4}}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0})^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0})\leq\frac{1}{d^{4}}\left\|\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}\right\|^{2}\leq d^{-0.8}
,
and

\frac{1}{d^{4}}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}=\frac{\operatorname{Tr}({\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}^{2})^{2}}{d^{4}}\mathbf{1}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}\mathbf{1}\lesssim d^{-2}

with probability at least
1-O(d^{-1/2})
, because of (
109
) and Lemma
37
. Moreover, the last term can be bounded by Cauchy-Schwartz inequality:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d^{4}}|(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0})^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}|\leq\frac{1}{d^{4}}\left((\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0})^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}(\boldsymbol{\mu}-\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0})\right)^{1/2}\left(\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}_{0}\right)^{1/2}\lesssim d^{-1.4}.

(237)

Then we complete the proof of the lemma.
∎

Lemma 39
.

Under the assumptions of Theorem
1
and Assumption
2.2
,
we have with probability at least
1-O(d^{-1/48})
,

{\boldsymbol{b}}_{0}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}})^{-2}{\boldsymbol{b}}_{0}\lesssim d^{-0.8}

Recall the definition of
\widetilde{\boldsymbol{b}}_{0}
in (

). We have

\displaystyle\left\|(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{b}}_{0}\right\|^{2}\leq\penalty\

\displaystyle 2\left\|(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}})^{-1}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{b}}_{0}-{\boldsymbol{b}}_{0})\right\|^{2}+2\left\|(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}})^{-1}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{b}}_{0}\right\|^{2}

(238)

\displaystyle\lesssim\penalty\

\displaystyle n\cdot\max_{i\in[n]}|\widetilde{b}_{0,i}-b_{0,i}|^{2}+\mathbf{1}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}})^{-2}\mathbf{1}+\frac{1}{d^{4}}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}\lesssim d^{-0.8},

(239)

with probability at least
1-O(d^{-1/48})
, where we use Lemma
34
, (
109
), Lemma
37
and Lemma
38
.
Similarly, by Lemmas
34
,
37
, and
38
, and (
109
), we have

\displaystyle{\boldsymbol{b}}_{0}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}})^{-1}{\boldsymbol{b}}_{0}\lesssim\penalty\

\displaystyle\left\|(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}})^{-1/2}(\widetilde{\boldsymbol{b}}_{0}-{\boldsymbol{b}}_{0})\right\|^{2}+\left\|(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}})^{-1/2}\widetilde{\boldsymbol{b}}_{0}\right\|^{2}

(240)

\displaystyle\lesssim\penalty\

\displaystyle n\cdot\max_{i\in[n]}|\widetilde{b}_{0,i}-b_{0,i}|^{2}+\mathbf{1}^{\top}(\boldsymbol{K}+\lambda{\mathbf{I}})^{-1}\mathbf{1}+\frac{1}{d^{4}}\boldsymbol{\mu}^{\top}\boldsymbol{K}_{\lambda}^{-1}\boldsymbol{\mu}\lesssim 1,

(241)

with probability at least
